LADIES' LETTER.

lIIE DEAR NELL/E,

I hear, and indications are by no means wanting, that hats are to be worn very large and of pic- turesque shape, trimmed chiefly with long sweep- ing ostrich feathers, and these are delicately tinted. A pale rose drap de feutre hat, plateau shape, has an ostrich feather of deeper shades, over a foot in length, sweeping round underneath the raised felt from right to left, drooping on the hair behind. These drap de feutre chapeaux de style in pinky shades are the heighth of the fashion. The other prevailing style is the marquise or tricorne, but larger, and the upturned brims more spreading than of yore. These are very stylish in white or pale grey felt, with the edges of the brims bound with dark-brown or black velvet and trimmed with an upright, bushy, stiff military aigrette ; sometimes a scarf of old lace is draped round the crown. Birds, wings, and plumage predominate on many models, the whole bird being used poised flat on the crown, the head and beak to the back.

It is too early as yet to forecast what will be the leading styles in evening dress, but it is almost safe to predict that the Empire modes will pre- dominate. They fall hi so well with the present craze for the picturesque, and they display to per- fection the beautiful embroidered stuffs which are

being prepared for evening wear. At the present moment, at country houses and shooting rarties, most, of the younger women wear plain clear muslin& A charming blouse is soft blue cash- mere, cut with a seam under each arm and on the shoulder, and mounted on a tight-fitting lining, over which is a vest of white lawn, with side- pieces of embroidered silk. There is a simple white silk blouse which needs no lining, the silk being mounted on to a square-cut yoke of insertion, with a shaped centre-piece down the front. There is one of the new boleros, tight- fitting, with a shaped belt attached covered with braiding. The fronts are scalloped out in battle- ments, with turn-down collar, whilst the sleeves are inserted under a scalloped armhole in quite a new manner.

It seems despite the change of other fashions that the long skirt is to remain with us. Not so long as it has been, certainly, but, nevertheless, not nearly short enough to be either comfortable or cleanly wear. Trimmed skirts will be rather less in favour, however. Nine out of 10 of the new models are plain, but a few are tucked from the waist to the top of the added flounce, which is completely covered with close tucks. The fashion- able skirt is very wide at the hem and has the inverted box-pleat at the back. Smooth cloth and Tough frieze are the two popular fabrics for autumn gowns, especially in coppery reds and browns, heather-purples, and greys. A copper-red cloth looks extremely well made up as a plain skirt and bolero—this style of jacket shows no signs of going out of fashion—with collar and cuffs of Chinese embroidery in dull blue and gold, and a blouse of deep ecru soft silk or tucked chiffon, with a gold tissue belt. An equally good effect can be achieved by having a plainly-stitched bolero without revers, with a blouse of silk in which blue, red, and gold are mingled. Most of the autumn boleros have wide bell sleeves, a minority displaying tight onog with deep mousquetaire cuffs.

I have seen a grey check skirt and plain grey -cloth coat, forming a costume suitable for fishing. The colour is kept in dark tones of grey for this reason, as the fish are so easily startled by any more obtrusive tint. The collar and cuffs of the little, tight-fitting coat are in leather, and the kilted skirt has a useful flap-pocket on either side. The knitted tam o'shanter is grey, like the dress, .and is lifted on the left side with a dark grey plume. The skirt might be short, whether in- tended for wear when fishing or for the moors or mountaineering. Homespun is an excellent mate- rial for all such gowns, being light of weight and, generally, in the natural colours of the wool. Most girls and women carry skout with them an unnecessary and injurious weight of skirt. This can easily be avoided by choosing a light woollen stuff and avoiding a heavy lining. Silk is by no means the best fabric for lining skirts, though it is the most fashionable. A light but strong muslin is often used now by dressmakers, and has several advantages over the more usual silk. It not only wears better, but adapts itself more readily to the folds of the material it lines. Your loving %TENNIS,

ALWAYS USH McDOITGALL'S PATBZIT SELF—RAISINI

WOMEN'S CHAI.

Queen Wilbelmina, in opening her Parliament, ex- pressed the hope that it will be possible to do something for the suppression of he evils of gambling, and drink- inn, and for the general improvement of the moral con- dition of her people. The present German Emperor has, at sundry times, tnought to rnake his people moral by Act of Parliament, and it will probably ne said that the young Queen is not likely to succeed where so many older, and more experienced rulers and statesmen have failed in the past. It will however effect some good. Our late Queen never actively interfered in the affairs of Parliament, but she ever encouraged all that was best by her example and the purity of her Court, and, by' these and every means, Queen Wilhelmina is evidently determined to do what she can to elevate her people. Mrs Rooeve,t,sa ho now becomes first lady in America, and mistress of the White Rouse. is the second wife of the new President of the Until Statss. Toe first Mra RoAevelt was a Miss Alice Lee of Boston, who died three years after her marriage leaving a daughter, now about eleven or twelve years old. The present Mrs Roosevelt, formerly Miss Edith Carow, was a great per- sonal friend of the first wife, and has four young children. She is a highly intellectual woman, speaking several languages. and has, like most american women travelled a goad deal. " The impossibility of dressing on £l,OOO a year "is the subject of an article in one of the current magazines. Many women who go out continually, and have to dress well, would be only too glad of this income to live upon, and such could have but little patience with the article in question. It would, of course, be quite an inadequate , sum for a leading society woman, to whom £lOO guineasl for an evening gown, and £2O to £3O for a simple house dress, is a very ordinary, everyday price. But these women are comparatively few and far between, as are also those of rur sex possessed of £l,OOO a year pin money. The vast majarity of us have to be satisfied with a very much more moderate allowance, and, to our credit be it said, manage, generally speaking. to Present to the VI Gild a very creditable appearance upon it. As a trimming for simple morning hats, sat silks still prevail over glace and firm makes, but the spotted silk scarves are no more. The fatal " tbree-farthings" appended to their price in the sales, has lost them tbe favour cf the select few, by bringing them within the reach of the many. Their place is taken by cut silk squares, almost as soft as the foulards and handsomer in design. The new ribbons, which are appearing on many an up-tc-date hat, are rather wide, of soft and good quality, and brocaded in the most delicate colours that harmonise with anything. Ruffles are again well to the fore. Not the firm closet ones with which we are familiar, but wide, loosely flying Inns, more like fichus than toa.. Many of these ruffiss are in the form of ruches, which like a cape, spread out Over the top of the sleeves, and in front fall down on each side, almost to the feet. These turns not only appear in black and white, tut in gauze chiffon and net of many colours, often blended together, to give a pretty iridescent effect. Large full ruches such as these, take a great deal of material to build them, and are therefore somewhat Costly. They arc however finding much favour with these who like to wear such things as are not likely to be too generally seen. In belts there is already great variety and we are promised a revival this autumn, of the Swiss shape. This is being arranged of three or more bands of rather narrow ribbon, running closely parallel under the arms,' and gsadually expanding, hack and front, to at least three times its narrowest width. Where the ribbons are thus drawn apart.they are sawn down to an upright whalebone, of the required height, covered with the same ribbon. Wide soft ribbon is also being used to manufacture the SWIPEI belt. It is expanded back and front by boning, and is allowed to narrow itsslf at the sides. Not quite does this belt meet, and the deficit is made up by adding streamers of ribbon which crosa and tie in front. White muslin cushion-covers continue in vogue, and haptily so, for they work quite a transformation, at small expense. The simplest are of white bock-muslin, rather deeply frilled. the next stage is when they are more or less elaborately embroidered, and the acme of effect is arrived at by muslin cushion covers, encrusted handsomely with lace. These latter may run us to great expense, but for utility and appearance also, de quite as well when not too ambitious, These delicate covers are for the boudoir and drawing room, the plainer OAPS for the bedroom and morning roam, and the simplest of all are of great value for throwing over faded, and dingy specimeas. One item is imperatively necessary, they must be absolutely clean, white, and fresh. Cot covers are made very much as are the cushion. covers, and on these a considerable amount of work is expended. Nothing is too smart to spread over baby's bed, or carriage, and so the dairtlest of work in white floss silk appears an a simple muslin ground. There is not much difficulty about the work for thosejwho possess cool fingers, for there are many suitable patterns to he had. The design selected is fastened underneath the muslin, and the needle, threaded with fines silk, pick• its way over this, in and out the mesh of the te Vile, the ends of silk being left fairly long and faetened off securely on the wrong side when the pattern is removed. When the equinoctial gales sit in, we should once more be careful of colds and coughs, for our own sake and that of others. There is no worse infliction than anyone with a bad cold, and many believe that we only take these complaints from others, just the same as in the case of the more serious infectious diseases. It would however, take a let ot scientific theory to con- vince many of us that we could not ;lust as easily rake cold by getting over warm and an after chill, and it is just at this time of the year, when the days are warm and the nights cod and chilly, that we are most liable to these rieks. For these autumn days, clothing of fairly light tut warm materials, is the moat suitable, as purely winter garnymts are almost as risky as those of summer, seeing that one is likely to get overheated in them. It is different with bsets and shoes, and now that we must expect the autumn rain., the thin-soled variety should be strictly discarded, when taking our walks abroad.

It is not so long since the " mere man " wed to pooh- pooh feminine feats of swimming and billiard-playing. Now these recreations are almost as IsPenly followed by the one sex as hy the other. The annual competitions at the Bath and Ravensbeurne Clubs bnow that swim- ming is greatly increasing in popu!arity amongst fashionable women, and there is the Austrian lady who stiU hr,pos to swim the Channel tetween Dover and Calais. There is also no obvious reason why the billiard champion should net te a woman seeing that delicacy of touch rather than strength is the chief qualification. In any case billiard playing is increasing at the high- timed ladies' oinks. ar dat some of them, especially at the Sandringham Club, some fine displays by titled

Several people have 'been writing to a London paper with suggestions for a universal language, but it is to erably safe to say that no such idea is likely to be put into practice in time to be of any use to the present generation. A good many folks thought that Volapuk was destined to fulfil all the requirements, but Volapuk however useful it may have been, was not striaingly beautifully, and for that, or some other reason, it is DOW seldom heard of. Meanwhile the penalty of Bs bel remains, and is likely to remain yet for a good ma ny years.

THE EXHIBITION OUTDONE.

Glasgow Really Excited.

An occurrence in Glasgow has just come to light which it is safe to say will arouse deeper interest to thousnds than all the wonders of the Expoiiition. The first details were published by a well-known journalist who received full confirmation at the facts at 31, Belfort-street, the shop of Mr. M'Garva. ANOTHER CONSCIENTIOUS OBJECTOR. ---- The Magistrates' Duty.

"Not to Find Fault with the Law but to

Administer it."

OUR FIRST AUTUMN SALE!

Another conscientious objector to vaccination made his appearance at the Warrington Police Court on Tues- day to apply for an exemption order. His name V 7.48 Arthur Cotton,ll7, Liverpool-road, and he applied for a vaccination exernptionfcertificate. The certificate of the birth of the child haying been produced, the Clerk asked the applicant to tell the Bench why ho wished the order. Applicant : Because from what I have seen I believe vaccination to be injuriods to the health of children. Alderman Hutchinson . What have you seen ? Applicant : I lost one of any brothers through vac- cination. Dr Fox : How long after he was vaccinated 1 Applicant t A very few months afterwards. Dr Fox (shaking his head) : I suppose if he had fallen downstairs alter he had been vaccinated you would have said the death was through vaccination. Applicant : I cannot say that. Is reply to Alderman Hutchinson. applicant said he bad been vaccinated, and as far as he knew had never suffered in consequence. Dr Fox : Do you recollect how the symptoms mani- fested themselvos in the case of your brother ? Applicant did not. Alderman Shaw Green : Have yeu had any other experience ? Applicant : A very great friend of mine told me that before his child was vaccinated it was very healthy, that afterwards its health was never the same, and that it died. Dr Fox : The applicant is an anti-vaccinator, and you may talk till you are black in the face and he will be of the same opinion still. A'derman Shaw Green : Those of us who have bad a wide experience of sanitary matters realize the serious respovsibility the applicant is putting upon the district. tOr Fox (to the applicant) : You have a duty to dis- charge to the State as well as to yourself. We have to protect other people. Have you thought of that Applicant : Yes, tut I consider vaccination injurious in any case, and that is my objection. Alderman Shaw Green thought that perhaps on reflection Mr Cotton would be in a wieer state of mind, and therefore he would suggest that his application should not be granted that time. Dr Fox : But it is the law that we should grant those arntications when the applicant has a conscientious objection. Alderman Shaw Green : I think if there is any chance of the applicant changing his mind we should give him that onportunity. Dr Fox :It is the law, that is wrong and they take advantage of it. We are here to administer the law and not to find fault with it. Alderman Shaw Green : I thit.k in the interests of the community we have a perfect right to make sugges- tions but you are chairman, of course. Dr Fox : VVe have no alternative but to grant the application. The application was ultimately granted.

In a corner of the room wad a screen, quaintly painted in imitation of,Wattean ; and from behind the screen was visible an old-fashioned escritoire. From where I sat I could only see it in part, but the design was familiar to me, and I was sou, now I came to see it, that it was either the sacrztvire which had stood in the octagon room at Arrish Mell Court, or else one exactly like it. I yondered very much whether Roger (Who owned the esrrituire, in common with everyttaing else that had been Lady Cope's) had really attached enough value to the old piece of furnithre to transplant it here, and, if so, what had been the underlying motive. I said nothing of all that was in my thoughts to Sintra Leigh, however; and as a French clock on the mantel softly chimed the half-hour after nine, I rose, announcing that I would like to go to bed. My companion received the proposal with uncon- cealed relief; and indeed, for the past hour, since we had left the dinner-table, our conversation had been the reverse of lively. She went with me as far as the door of my bed- room, pointing out her own door further down the ball. 'I shewed you which was my room during your tour of inspection this afternoon," she said, "but I will remind you now, in case you should need anything in the night. Not that you are likely to lie awake, 1 trust. Compose your mind with the resolve to do as your best friend, Roger Cope, desires, and you will sleep peacefully enough.".

"This is certainly not a bad place to be mistress of," I answered, diplomatically. A moment more and I was alone. Still, there was nothing to do save to wait until the house should be quiet for the night. As to what should happen after that, my ideas were vague, but all my thoughts turned towards the Secret. There was no clock in the room, but I had wound and set the watch which had come back from the pawnbroker's, add when it told half-pest ten I looked out into the corridor. It was still lighted, but very dimly. The gas had been lowered since I came to my bedroom, and a faint yellow star, shining through the glass globe, redeemed the passage from darkness.

For a few moments I listened, but there wasifot a sound to be heard. Evidently the house (had gone to sleep for the night, and if I could hope to do anything, I might as: well make the attempt now as later.

I took a candle in a tall silver stick from my mantel, and a box of matches, but I did not mean to light the candle unless it were needed. Without a light, I might hide in the shadows, in ease of a sadden alarm, while with it I should at once become a conspicuous figure for prying eyes. I walked along the corridor until I reached the stairs, and then I cautiously ascended the two flights which lay between my floor and the third storey. Now and again a board squeaked under my slippered foot, and I stopped, with a beating heart, peering fearfully into the dusk. But the noise had only seemed loud in my own fears; no one else had heard, and no door opened to frame a peeping face. At last I reached the third floor, which was also the highest. Here also there was a faint light, and I could see in the distance the door of which Roger Cope was said to keep the key. So far, everything had been easy—far easier than I dared to hope it would be in the end. I decided that I would go to the door and knock very softly. If I was answered by a voice from the other side, I should feel that my theory regarding last night was correct.

Suddenly, as I tiptoed towards the door' it seemed to me that I heard someone speak. My heart gave a great bound, and, almost involuntarily, I shrank into the thick shadow of a great old- fashioned clothes-press which stood half-way down the hall. Hardly had I crouched into the corner '.ben the door towards which I had hem bound opened. A bright light shone out from inside, silhouetting the tall black figure of Sintra Leigh against a yellow background. In crossing the threshold she turned and looked back, speaking to someone in the room. "I will bring it to you in less than ten minutes," she said, in an ordinary tone, without trace of agitation. "Be patient till then." . _

She took a step, forward, but a faint murmur from the, person unseen caused her again to pause. I could not hear the question, if question it was,but her answer reached me distinctly. "I cannot help that. After what happened last night, it is your own fault. But though the door must be locked after this, for the next night or two at all events, when you are left alone,. you are in no sense a prisoner. As soon as you ,are well enough, if you , choose to leave this house, and there is any other place where you would rather live, you have' only to say so. I catne here for your sake alone. And everything I have done since coming has been , for your sake. It appears to Inc that you are very well off here. But we will talk about it again when I have brought you the wine." She closed the door, and once more the cerridor was dim. But I could see that, she was bending down, and I could hear the fitting of a key into a lock. If I could escape her sharp eyes, in Inv dark, but otherwise sadly insecure biding-place, after all this seeining contretemps might work out for good. Evidently the key of this door was not in Roger Cope's possession, but in Shure Leigh's. Now, if she would only, only leave it in the lock I Was it possible that she would do so? For an instant she appeared to hesitate. I could guess what was in her thoughts. She was wonder- ing whether there could be the smallest imprudence in letting the key remain where it was for the few moments of her intended absence. Before I could have counted twelve she had decided that she might safely trust it there, and, turning the key round with a faint grating noise, she hurried away. My only hope of escaping her quick eyes lay in the sheltering shadow of the big clothes-press as I stood huddled into the angle it made in the wall, and the fact that the woman had no suspicion of my near- ness. Probably she would have locked me into my room if she had not disliked proceeding to extreme measures by removing the key from the inside. Or perhaps she had felt confident that, after. the alarm of the preceding; night, I would not dream of venturing out after dark. She had reason to be secure in the knowledge that I could not leave the house, and she might have reflected that vague curiosity alone was not a strong enough motive to lure me from safety. These thoughts darted through my head as she came nearer, and gave me hope. Fortunately, she carried no candle, and she had just come from a room brightly lighted, so that the darkness must have appeared more dense to her than it did to me. I was half afraid that she might hear Inv heart beating or the rustle of My gown as it r, s and fell with the throbbing of Hay bosom, for the sound was loud in my own ears. I hardly dared look at her as she approached, lest my eyes should attract hers with some subtle inagnetisnn, yet I could not force myself to turn them from the tall, swiftly advancing figure. It, was ali over, however, in less than a minute, and she had gone by without even glancing towards the clothes-press. The sense of relief was so great when she had actually passed, that I grew giddy, almost faint. But there was no time for analysing my own sensations. Not a second was to be lost if I meant to do the daring thing which had presented itself as an enurement to my mind. The woman in black ha'd said that she vimuld be away less than ten minutes. I might, then, count on at least five clear minutes in which to work.

1 • Peering out from my hiding-place, I only- waited ' until Sintra Leigh's head had idisappeared down the well of the staircase. Then .1 flew to the door of the room she had left, and unlocked it. ,-I was too much excited now to be afraid, or I might have hesitated in dread of what I should se on the other side. But I did pot hesitate. I turned the key and opened the door without knocking. What I saw was a commonplace, comfortably furnished bedrocm. . A large reading-lamp itood on 1 a table near a huge canopied bed, and in the bed, supported by banked-up pillows, reclined a man. 1 ,The first glance told me that he was the original ' of the third portrait in the library downstairs, and I felt ar less surprise at this discovery than I should had I seen a strange face. There are so many things you could tell me, if you would; and perhaps I could tell you some things that you would care to hear, if only you'd listen." He fell back among the pillows, groaning, his hands, thin and yellow as claws, hiding his face. For an instant I had forgotten that at almost any moment the woman might break in upon us. but now I remembered, with a shock. I ran to the bed and bent over the man who lay there. "There are other things I can tell you—things that have been kept from you, perhaps," I said. "Do you wish to hear them?" "Yes, yes " he moaned. "There isn't time to tell you now, for we are liable to be interrupted. Is Sintra Leigh your sister ?" The man looked me in the face once more and nodded, without speaking. ",She wished to keep us apart. When I asked about this room to-day she said that it was a store- place and Sir Roger Core kept the key. When she comes back, if she finds me here, she will send me away. She is stronger than I, and she can call the servants to aid her if she chooses ; I can't resist. When she comes back, she will think that she must have failed to lock the door, or perhaps she will turn the key round and not know that it has been tampered with. If I hide in this room, so that I can talk to you when she has gone away again, will you promise to keep the secret? "Yes," the man answered. "She has tricked me, and she deserves to be trieked in return." I could not be sure whether or no he would keep his word. But I determined to take the risk. Al worst, Sintra Leigh would hardly dare to kill me, whatever might be her desire for revenge should she find me out. "list!" the man's voice broke into my thoughts. "I heard something in the distance. She's on her way back." Without a word I flew to the window nearest. It was covered with heavy curtains that had beer drawn together and fell in straight folds to the floor. Behind them I found that the window was partly open, to let in the mild air of the June night. If the curtains moved, Sintra Leigh might think that they were stirred by the breeze. There was a deep embritsure, and, gathering my skirt closely about me, I knew that, unless the woman in black should be seized by a whim or I was betrayed by her brother, I stood in no immediate danger of discovery. The curtains had scarcely ceased to quiver after being drawn into place by me, when I heard the door open. I half expected Sintra Leigh's first words to be an expression of surprise at finding the door unlocked, but, instead, she began quietly to speak about a bottle of port which she had fetched from downstairs. No doubt she had done what I had suggested—turned the key without becoming aware that someone had been before her. I had listened eagerly to this discussion, for "L divined that the invalid's request had been as much with a view to my Convenience as his own. She went on the stage and became an opera-singer. She was but eighteen, your age to-day, and in the first company which she joined was a man whose father had been a gipsy. He was the leading tenor, and he was as handsome as she was beautiful, though a wild, worthless fellow. They married secretly. Then a time came when the marriage had to be announced. Our mother left the opera troupe, but our father stayed. Sintra and I were born. The husband and wife never saw each other again. He was killed in a railway accident, and as our mother had been disowned by her parents she was penniless. We were left with an old nurse while she went back to the stage, and for two or three years we were neglected, growing up like weeds in an uncared-for garden. "Meanwhile, our mother met a young baronet, named Henry Cope, who fell in lovp with her beauty, and they were married.. We were brought to this house as our home; this very room was our nursery, and we were happy enough until Vincent Cope, our half-brother, was born. "With his birth everything was changed. Oil step-father, who had never liked us, turned against us almost with hatred; even our mother took her love away from the poor gipsy twins, who recalled the sordid past, and gave it to the child who was born to good fortune. Sintra and I were devoted to each other; she perhaps caring more for me than I for her—as is the way sometimes with a brother and sister; and she hated the new baby as only a gipsy child can hate. One day, when Vincent was about two years old, she struck him, so that he fell and cut his forehead, And this act sealed our fate.

"Our step-father pronounced life unendurable while we remained under his roof, and we were to be sent 'away. The question was, where, for we were almost too young to go to a boarding school; besides, we were so inseparable that it would have been cruel to part us ; and neither our mother nor her husband had the active wish, I think, to be cruel.

"A circumstance 'trifling in itself decided our whole future. A child, Ermyntrude D'Esterre, cousin of the Copes, .wbo had been brought here on a visit by her parents, had taken a great fancy to the two gipsy children. When her father and Mother, whose home was at Arrish Mell Court (which yon must know well) heard that we were in disgrace with our step-father, they invited us to pay them a visit and share the 'teachings of their daughter's governess. We managed to win our way, somehow, into the hearts of our protector*, instead of paying a. mere visit we continued to live at Arrish Mell Court and were as much children of

the house as Ermyntrude herself. "So we: grew up together. I was not a strong boy, and, instead of being sent to a public school I had a tutor. Ermyntrude and Sintra and I were never an hour apart; and not a cloud arose between us until Sintra and I were seventeen, Ermyntrude thirteen. Then, mature beyond my years, I knew 'that I loved Ertnyntrude, not with the brother-love I calmly gave Sintra, but a feeling very different. AI last I was going to travel with my tutor, and before leaving I told Ermyntrude what was in my heart for her. She vowed that she loved me, too, and promised that when she grew up she would not marry anyone but me. I wanted her to prove this promise. I wanted tribe sure that she belonged to me; and I persuaded her to let me tattoo on her

srm the mark of a heart, whichw as ti be tne fay- timile .sf one on mine. _

"She was afraid and unwilling, but consented at ; and I made the hearts as best I knew how, in my amateurish way, with a purple ink which I bought from a man I knew. "While I was fashioning the heart on Ermyn- trude's arm, copying each detail from my own, with my sleeve rolled up that I might' have the' model ever before my eyes, Sintra looked in upon us. She was furious with jealousy that Ermyntrude' should share something with me that was denied to her. From that moment I believe that she hated Ermyntrude. "When I had gone away on my travels, Sintra made a heart on her own arm, and told Ermyntrude that it had been done by my desire, but that was not true. I knew nothing of its existence until Ermyntrude wrote me that, since Sintra now wore the symbol, Shy had ceased to value hers as before. "For years, though' the two families lived in Dorsetshire, not fifty miles distant from each other, we saw little of Vincent Cope, for our mother died, and the boy and his father went abroad together. It was while I was travelling with my tutor that we met—my half-brother, his Lather and I. Only the' day after the meeting, which took place in Switzer- land, Sir Henry Cope had an accident in climbing. He lay ill for a few weeks, and finally died, 'so- that Vincent became Sir Vincent, though he was. but a boy. "My tutor,-who was a good fellow, made him— self very useful to Vincent, and we all went back to England together, taking the body of the dead baronet with us. After this Vincent and I remained friends, though Sintra's continued dislike of him kept us from accepting his invitation to share his home. Arrish Mell was the home of our hearts, and *our, friends there seemed no more desirous of sending us away than we were of going. But Vincent and I went up to Oxford together; and in those days he had our three portraits painted, to be hung in the library in this house, where you may have seen them. "Vincent was sent down from Oxford on account of an audacious prank. I left at about the same time because of a severe accident at football. He went abroad with friends; I back to be nursed into health at Arrish Mell Court.

"By this time I shewed signs of latent talent as an artist. Ermvntrude's people. who were simple- And unmercenar'y, believed that I might make my way in the world, and when they found out that we had loved each other for years they .consented to our engagement. _ _ _

"Then,-a-3 Ern yntrude and I both longed for Italy, and she would have plenty of money for us both, it was arrange that we should be married without delay. Ermyntrude was twenty, I twenty- four. _

"I was perfectly happy, despite Sintra's jealous depressions My heart was full of good feeling for all the world, and I was delighted when Vincent wrote that he would come and be best man at the wedding. "Well, he came, and arrived at Arrish Melt Court, where he was to pay alkit, only a week before the day set for the mEriage. He was a. handsome and brilliant young fellow, not much older than Ermyntrude. They had not met for some time, and I might have seen a danger signal in their eyes when they looked at each other. But I loved Ermyntrude so passionately that I did not dream her feeling for me might be nothing stronger than the affectionate habit of years, which I had thought her to misunderstand.

"When Vincent had been in the house for three days she begged me to release her from ,her engage- ment, eleventh hour though it was, alleging no reason except that she did not care for me as sho had thought. I was half-mad, and I refused te give her the freedom she asked for. She seemed as much mine as if she were already my wife; she had been mine ever since I put my heart on her arm, hers on mine. "On the morning of the day that should have given her to me for ever she *as gone—she and Vincent together. They went to Scotland, and were married almost before we had time to guess what had happened. "For weeks I lay at death's door with brain fever. Sintra nursed me through the illness as no. one else could have done. But I was never the• same man again. The blood in my veins seemed turned to gall. I believed that I hated V'rmyn— trude as much as once I had loved her I and I thought of nothing but some way of making her feel as I felt.

"Perhaps if her parents, who had been like• Father and mother to me, had lived, I would after ill have done nothing against their daughter. But while Sintra and I travelled, trying to forget, they died. A year or two later, my chance came. and —with Sintra's promptings in my ears—l teak it.". (To be concluded.)

THE FREE CHURCHES.

THE GIRLS' GUILD ORGANISING SECRETARY.

Mrs. Charlotte Benham has been appointed t Secretary of the Girls' Guild Committee of the National Council. Mrs. Benham has been -engaged in religious work for a great many years, and has especially distinguished herself in connection with the Sunday School Union. She enters upon her new duties with the greatest enthusiasm, and with many qualifications for the work, not the least of which is her ardent sympathy with girls and her interest in their lives. We understand that Mrs. Benham's im- mediate duties will be to assist in the organiing of Guilds all over the country, and to nurture them during their infant existence, and to watch over their careers with guidance and suggestion. It is expected that, during the Autumn and Winter, Guilds will be formed all over the • country in connection with the National Free Church Council. This Guild movement is an integral part of the work of the National 'Council.

===== 0002601_19010927_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
MARRYING AND BAPTISED PERSONS.

A Baptist country pastor has written to us, -saying that the other day he performed the marriage ceremony for a couple who had been refused it by the High Church vicar, because they had not been baptised. The vicar ex pressed his readiness to grant their wishes if they would submit to the ceremony of baptism, but they refused, and hence the sequel. We are informed by Mr. J. Bamford Slack, 8.A., the legal adviser of the National Pree Church Council, that it is 'illegal for a clergyman to refuse to marry unbaptised persons, provided they are his parishioners.

===== 0002601_19010927_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
NO FREE CHURCH WOMAN NEED APPLY.

In a Yorkshire local paper there appeared re- cently an advertisement for a chief nurse at the North Riding Asylum. It stated that the occupant of the post must be experienced and a member of the Church of England. This is a matter which requires looking into, not only on its own • account, but because there is a suspicion that . similar stipulations are made by asylum authorities all over the country. The asylum in question is supported by the county rates, . and the ratepayers are certainly not all Angli- cans, and indeed in that part of the country the probability is that the majority of them are Nonconformists. If these offices are to be limited to Anglicans, surely Nonconformists should not be called upon to pay rates in sup- port of them.

===== 0002601_19010927_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
PRESIDENT MeKINLEY.

Nonconformists may, without offence, claim to have had a special interest in President McKinley. He was a Methodist, and in his temperament, disposition, and manner of life, strongly recalled the old Puritans. His death occurring while the Methodist CEcumenical Conference was meeting in London had a solemnising effect upon that ceremony. The great calamity may be said to have covered its entire proceedings. Dr. Frank M. Bristol, who was the minister of President McKinley's church at Washington, was among the delegates - and spoke in the highest terms of the great- hearted and simple-hearted statesman. Presi- dent McKinley attended church regularly, and liked to sit in his own pew unobserved. It is interesting to note that his successor is also a man of deep religious character, and an active local preacher among the Methodists and other communities.

===== 0002601_19010927_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
FREE CHURCH SCHOOLS.

The October Free Church Chronicle contains a brief but interesting ar.ticle On the Free Church Schools which have just been opened in Bournemouth. In one part of that town there, is a deficiency of unsectarian accommoda- tion, and, unhappily, there is no prospect of a School Board. 1/1 these ciroumstances, the Free Churchmen have set about the task of

!erecting schools for themselves. They are still In considerable debt, but they have been opened, and the attendance iS now over 300. The example of Bournemouth may be com- mended to other Free Church Councils throughout the country which find themselves in a similar situation. The article has two rather striking illustrations.

'-THE CHURCH CONGRESS AND, NONCONFORMITY.

The Church Congress is to meet at Brighton this year early in October. We are pleased to hear that the Congress has consented to receive an address from the Sussex Federation of Free Church Councils, of which Mr. J. Compton Rickett, M.P., is the President. Mr. Rickett is a very fitting person to convey the good wishes of Brighton and Sussex Free ChUrchmen to' Anglican visitors. .While himself a sturdy Nonconformist, he is bound by intimate family ties to the Anglican Church, and he very fre- quently emphasises the statement that the Free Church Federation movement has nothing but goodwill for the Anglican Church as a spiritual society.

FREE CHURCHMEN AND PRESIDENT MeKINLEY. There was a crowded congregation at the Memorial Service to President McKinley, which was held at the City Temple under the auspices of the National. Free Church Council. The service throughout, which was of a united character, Was most impressive, Dr. 'Parker was the preacher, and his sermon was most striking, the subject of which being Anarchism, dealing with "The Beginning and the Springs of Evil." He toolitas his text, "But let none of you suffer as a murderer, o. as a thief, Gr. as --an evil-doer, or as a busy-body in other men's matters." The. Revs. Dr. Monro-Gibson, Dr. Townsend, Thomas Law, J. Scott Lidgett, M.A., H. B. Kendall,. 8.A., and Gipsy Smith also assisted in the service. The beautiful solo from " The Messiah" was delightfully rendered by Miss Stanley Lucas.

===== 0002601_19010927_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
DR. CLIFFORD.

Dr. Clifford was to have taken part in the United Free Church Memorial Service for the late President McKinley at the City Temple, but, owing to an unfortunate accident, he was kept away. We understand that Dr. Clifford rather seriously injured himself while coming downstairs in his house, but he is now making good progress, and very soon, we hope, will resume his normal activity. Dr. Clifford must be the most energetic man in the world. For- tunately, too, he possesses a good stock of health and a most buoyant, elastic disposition. Re can do the work of three men with joy and 'ease.

Operations Watched Through Field Glasses

Two more bookmakers appeared at the Wariington Police Court on Monday, and were fined the maximum penalty for frequenting streets for betting purposes. Considerable interest centred in the cases, the court room being crowded. The magistrates on the Bench were the Mayor (presiding), Alderman Heaton and Messrs Birchall, Hayward, ‘Vrieht and Thornton. They bad heard of late much about the soldier and his bravery, but let them not forget that there were no more brave soldiers in the army of humanity than amongst the ranks of the nurses, who went out from day to day to do battle with suffering and with the wants of the poor, not only in homes which were well ordered out in homes where dirt brought disease and where want hastened the progress of the disease. (Applause). Of all the changes in the 19.11 century—that wonderful century whicn had brought about so many improvements and so many things which did not seem to be improvements—one of the most remarkable was the chi nee in the status of the nurse. They would no eloute: remember having read of Dickens' immortal Mrs Gatrip, who was an example of the nurse in olden times, and not a caricature. But that state of things bad happily been altered, thanks to Florence Nightingale and others, and the status which the nursing profession enjoyed to—day enabled some of the best and noblest of women to be'ong to it and to be proud of that fact. (Applause). Toe nurse now spread healing and hght in many a poor and rich house, and the old state of things had been changed in an in- credibly short space of time. Instead of the old nurse in the shape of Mrs Gump, a terribly dirty person, they had now the light-fingered,light-footed,smiling,, cheerful ' nurse to whom so many owed a deep debt of gratitude. PUTTING THE CLOCK BACK;—An amusing scene took place lately at a performance of Wallace's " rid,eritana " by a provincial company. In one scene of this opera much depends upon a clock, under which the principal characters collect and sing a ditty concerning the onward march of time, which none can stay. The clock, however, which is worked by a " super " behind the stage,on this occasion failed to do its duty properly. and while the actors duly sang their comments on fleeting time and the rapid approach of the dread hour, the bands moved slowly backwards.

THE WARRINGTON DISMICI NURSING ASSOCIATION.

Annual Meeting.

The Council Chamber at the Town Hall was filled on Monday evening with ladies and gentlemen interested in the Warrington District Nursing Association on the occasion of the fourth annual meeting of the institution. Mr F Monks(the hon treasurer and founder of the associa- tion) was vored to the chair. ThEr3 were also present : —The Revs EC E Carleton and John Yonge. the Revs Fathers Whittle and Wilson, Alderman M 'ink., Mr J Dutton Filenlough (one of the bon secretaries), Drs Fox and Guest Gornall, and Messrs John Fairclough, R A Naylor, Gorge Thompson, George Wallingtoo, W Beswick, as well as a large numbA of ladies,including Mrs Allen Bright, who delivered an address on the benefits of the association.

THE COMMITTEE'S REPORT.

The fourth annual report of the committee was pre- sented as follows :—" The committee have much pleasure in submitting their report for the fou• th year's work, which continues to be very satisfactory. The thanks of the committee are due to all the subscribers and those public bodies whose contributions have made so welcome and substantial an addition to the funds. For details of work done the committee refer subscribers and others to the superintendent's report, which speaks for itself, and indicates the number and variety of the cases visited. A reference to the tressurei's report will show the necessity for new and increased financial sup- port if the society is to continue & work commensurate with the needs,of the town. During the year a sixth nurse (Miss Eaton) has been added to the staff, which is now very efficient and capable. The home was vi.ited tw Miss Armstrong, the official inspector of glen Victoria's Jubilee Institute for Nurses, on December 14th, and the secretary writes and states that tile result of her inspection was ' very satisfactory.' To the superintendent and nurses our warmest thanks are due for their untiring devotion to their duties and the efficient manner in which they are discharged."

ADDRESS BY MRS. ALLEN BRIGHT. Mrs. ALLEN BRIGHT (Liverpool) spoke in support of the resolution, and referred to the satisfactory character of the report. They had, however, the universal com- plaint so far as reoorta were conc.erned-w-rnore subscrip- tions were required. Anyone with a slight acquaint- ance of Warrington, sueh as she had had, must see that there most be a great need for sick nursling, as a great deal of poverty and distress existed. Unfortunately, in the Southern part of Lancashire, the inhabitants were prone to all kinds of lingering illnesces, which celled for alleviation. Charities in her opinion, be divided into two classes—those which did a great desl of good and very little harm, and those which did a great deal of harm and not much good. Amongst the latter she included indiscriminate giving, Sue would be afr...id to say how much money was thiown away by giving to the beggar at the door without any trouble being taken to investigate the case.No doubt excellent work was done in the industrial schools or training homes by the removal of children from dangerous surroundings, and by bringing them up so that they might be useful citizms ; but, on the other hand, there was this dis- advantage—they removed from the parents the last lingering spark of individual responsibility. It might 'be said that hospitals were open to no objection, but she had been told 147 her medical friends that those institutions were often .u.sed by those who could I well afford to Pay for the patient to be treated !at home, and the result was that there was ! a pauperizing of the people. In the case of ! the Nursing Association, however, they had a ' charity that was Fareguardc.d. The cases were sent directly by the doctor or with the leave of the doctor, and the medical profession had no cause of complaint. Therefore, she Might claim for this charity the rare dis- tinction of being entirely ben,floial, not only beneficial to the individual who fell under the nurse's care, but to the community at large, because they must not forgot Chat a patient that was promptly and properly attended to was the sooner convalescent ; sometimes it meant even the difference between life and death,because how- ever ski!fui a doctor might be he could do nothing in a serious illness unless he was supported by the purses. (Hear, hear, and epplause.) A knowledge of sanitation and the means of preserving health was spreading. Every nurse who went into the world to (to her duty,aS they all did, was a health missionary, and introduced a knowledge of sanitation where it was badly needed. She brought it sometimes to the flanges of the rich, where it was equally as much needed as in the houses of the poor. Ibis charity was carrying on one of tne best and most beneflCial works that any of God's creatures could engage in. She admitted that at first a prejudice did exist against the nurse, but when the patient knew her she was truly thankful for her services. There was one point to which she wished to draw particular attention, and that was that the life of the district nurse was a hard one. She was brought face to face with heartrending scenes, ignorance, want and terrible destitution. She bad to create order out of chaos, and bad to face a want of the barest necessities of life. She went forth bravely from day to day doing

The life of the nurse was a hard one. Apart from the phy- sical side, they always seemed to be living behind toe scenes, and saw the seamy side. They sometimes felt that everywhere there was sickness, dirt and drunken ness, and the result was that sometimes they became very depressed. At the Same than the knowledge the,: they were able to help the sick served to make their work much less hard. DEATH OF MR. WILLIAM DI GGLE

Warring:on has this week los', a citizen whose actions made him one of the best-known men in the tJwii. At rive o'clock on Saturday morning there oassed away at his residence in Wiliis-street, Mr William Diggie, at the ripe age of 90 years. On Saturday week Mr Diggle was retirin,7, for the night, when he slipped and fell part way d)wri the stairs, and was badly shaken and bruised about the head. He was at once put to bed, and attended by Dr Joseph, but deceased seemed to gradually sink, taking na muriihment of any kind, and died as stated above. Ile leaves twa daughters.

Mr Diggle was born at Pendleto"n, near Manches- ter. and served his time to dyeing, and was for some time in business for himself, but afterwards went to Rocadale, and was engaged by the firm of Messrs Kings, cotton manufacturereof that place. In the year 1861 he came to Warrington, being engaged as managing dyer to Messrs Armit- age and Rigby's, Csickhedge Mills, and re- mained with them invil about 17 years ago, when he retired from work. Whilst at the latter firm, as was his wont, he could never see that the plant for dyeing was perfect, and was always suggesting what be termed improvements. Whether they turned out to be so or not, did not transpire, but he always found a willing ear in the person of Mr Samuel Rigby, one of the partners of the firm, who had great faith in, and respect for ttr Diggle. This mania for improvements the de- ceased carried out with great vigour outside the firm, and with very rare exceptions, he was found opposing almost everything that everybody did, not excluding the Warrington Cirporation. Up to the last he was "dead " against the Corporation Water Scheme, and vowed that the Council bad made a great mistake in not adopting what be at one time recommended, " tapping the Liverpool supply." He was sent with others to London, aud was the means of getting a clause inserted in the Bill enatling the Corporation to do this. He never seemed weary of pointing out to business men in particular that at one time there were 13 cotton mills in the town, and that now there was only one. This he always attributed to the bard- teas of the water which, he said, was unfit for , manufacturing purposes. and to back this up he Carried about with him pi'sces of pipes, choked and corroded, as results of the hardness of the water. The deceased was strongly opposed to many other schemes, including the intercepting depots, the removing of the night-soil, the Manchester Ship Canal, which he declared would never pay a divi- dend (in the latter case it has proved ouly:too true.) Mr. 'Jiggle was fond of lecturing and of rousing public opinion upon local sub3ects. On two or three I occasions the deceased aspired to be a member of ' the Warrington Town Council, and although con- testing one of the wards, never received much sup- port. He was for a short time a memher of the Board of Guardians. He was instrumental in the building of the local "House of Parliament," which is situated in the park, and where the town's busi- ness is often discussed, and a verdict given. Although very eccentric in his views, tie was a good debater, and could always hold his own in argu- ment, and no doubt his suggestions in many instances were acted upon. lie was very highly respected by all parties, and he had some weighty Hess if only he could have moulded them into shape, His remains were interred at Pendleton Churchyard, where his wife and son lie buried. Inquest. Mr. Brighouse, the comity coroner, conducted the inquest at the Police Station on Monday even- ing. Mr W J Forshaw. solicitor, represented the , relatives of the deceased.

Joseph Heron, eon-in-law of the deceased, said he was a solicitor's clerk, residing at 3, Willis-street. , Deceased was 90 years of age last birthday,and was a retired foreman dyer, and resided with witness at the above address. About half-past nine on the , night of the 11th hut. he left the kitchen to retire to bed, and when a little wsy upstairs he fell back- wards to ths bottom. Witness sent for Dr Macarthy who found him suffering from bruises at the back ,of the head. Dr Joseph, his own doctor was called in, and attended him until five o'clock on Sunday morning, when he died. Owing to his great age deceased was very feeble.—ln answer to a juror witness stated that he thought the accident would have occurred if there had been band rails in the house. Deceased was very independent in his actions.

The jury returned a verdict of " Accidental death."

During the last few The American Rival. days there have been

several reminders of the activity of our commercial rivals. The pur- chase of OGDEN'S by an American Trust has creat- ed a stir in the tobacco trade, and shipping firms remain uneasy as to the intentions of Mr. Pine.- POINT Moneax. Add to this that the Yankees still give us a lead in the application of electricity, and that in the matter of railways we have to look to them not only for traction but also for most conveniences, and it will be realised how they are making the pace for us. During the present week, too, they have been reminding us that they are not too elated to learn. Special offers have been made to a number of Lancashire cotton operatives I to go to America, in order that the quality of the workers there may be improved. Some consider- able number of men from Lancashire had previ- ously been induced to take their experience to the new country, but it was felt that the leavening influence was not sufficient, hence this further movement. It is not in the Western Hemi- sphere alone that enterprise is being shewn. A representative body of ag ricalturists from South Russia and Siberia has this week left for England to study the English markets. Siberia, so long regarded by Britishers as a territory of gloom and depression, simply because it was asso- ciated in the public mind with the conviA estab- lishments which are mere dots on its expanse, is really a fertile country --the Canada of the East, it has been appropriately called—waiting to be developed. More than that, it is a country that must be developed rapidly, as the railway, now completed, largely for military reasons, will re- quire goods traffic even at low rates to find em- ployment for the staffs. In Austria, in every town where there is a chance of encouraging and improving any industry, money is being spent lavishly in technical instruction to that specific end. Germany is more laboriously if not quite so effectively working in the same way, and the Ger- man travellers surpass all others in their readiness to accommodate customers in foreign countries. It is, however, from the United States that competi- tion is most to be feared because machinery in the States is being carried to such perfection, and men and masters are equally alert. Enterprise there never hesitates at a considerabie loss in the first instance, with the object of ultimate gain. It may not be good for the stability of trade, for firms in America are less secure than in our own country; but it promotes business and is good for labour, and brings money into the coffers of the nation. On the other hand, too much may be said about the disinclination of British firms to meet the wants of small buyers abroad. So long as a reputation can be kept up, and the more profitable large business done, it would be throwing money away to split up the goods to be sold into a mall quantities just to meet the caprice rather than the convenience of buyers. In every walk of life the principle holds true that people gain by a reason- able degree of independence if they have brains to let or goods to sell. There is wisdom in meet- ing the exigencies of competition slowly; but whatever else is done, the quality of the article should be maintained, and every effort should be madeto meet the times in superiority of produc- tion. It is in that relation that the conservatism of British manufacturars stands them in bad stead.

Mr PINERO'S new play The Critics and the Drama, has rekindled fires of con- troversy over the drama Mr ARCHER, who claims to be a purist, applauds it as highly edifying; the critic of a society news- paper would place it in a rubbish heap, whence

===== 0002601_19010927_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
may be traced by the pext generation the history of the decay of ideals. Incidentally, the sharp differences of impression produced by the self"

_ same play on the same tight must strike every- one. The fact makes us shudder to think how events of past times have taken colour from th e

minds of the chroniclers. If such divergencies of judgment occur among educated, tolerant people

_ at the beginning of the twentieth century, what sort of reliatility is to be placed on the character

studies, for example, of early English rulers, or of the motives actuating social movements in the Middle Ages? But perhaps, after all it may be said that the present is a transition period in English drama, and as the different schools are afraid of losing ground, they are a little more vehement than they need be. Th 3 Ibsenites speak with a conscious exaggera- tion; the playgoers who say they want only to be amused do themselves an injustice. Both would be satisfied with a recognition of the scope the drama offers alike for the study of human life and as a complete relaxation. Each Lc afraid that one or the other function to which he gives the pre- ference will be ignored. The Ibsenites would be very sorry to have IBSEN varied only by:MAETEa-• LINCK ; the lovers of musical comedy are quite aware how too much of this sort of thing cloys. Mr PINERO'S problem plays, as delicate reflections of modern society, are altogether different front the morbid studies of those who want to make the stage a pulpit, just as they are different Irons the productions of those who would sterilise it of

thought. All that he does is to present pictures of certain aspects of life, and to leave them with his audience. He offers no moral, but leaves the play to work its own influence. On the other hand, his sense of truth forbids him from caricaturing his life either by happy or tragic Lendings. The worst that happens in the last scene of "Iris" is that $- Jewish millionaire smashes some ornaments on a, mantelshelf. It is a remarkable action in such s person, but it is quite natural, in all the circum- stances. What does, however, hold the audience under a spell is the appearance of Iris in her black dress and hat, going, in her helplessness, from her surroundings of luxury into the thirkness beyond. In its simplicity, and in the very refusal to pursue the subject further than to indicate by the patch of grey hair and a wanness of cheeks the bitter- ness of the experience, this culminating scene fe a stroke of the Lhighest art. One has only to re- flect on the inadequacy, not to say, commonness, of Mr BARRIE'S treatment of a similar subject, to realise how delicately the dramatist has to work upon such a theme. It is absurd to say that properly done such a play has not its value morally, or that it has not a• strong human in- terest. It is the old story of the hardness of the way of transgressors, out it is told in a very modern parable, without sermonising.

A fillip is given to the discussion on the cause of the disaster to the Cobra by the statement that a wounded whale has been found by some fisher- men floating on the surface of the water in the Wash. The question is asked whether the sink- ing of the destroyer may not have been due to a collision with this cetacean, thus the innocent cause of the loss of sixty lives and the only responsible in: his Majesty's navy fitted with turbine.engines. Of course, there are well-recorded cases in which these monsters of the deep have been vessel for mishaps, though a vessel like the Cobra should have been capable bf cutting through a whale of tilt* size to be met with in the German Ocean. Our fan fathers had considerable fear of them, and tbe."" are many quaint prints in which immense OW ceans, as well as giant octopi, are seen eng ulfi i ng whole vessels p their Jaws,with crews of mariners- That a fate even remotely resembling that depicted should have befallen the Cobra would be strange i indeed, and, n the absence of positive evidence, the theory is. not likely to command official sanc- tion. Happily—if anything can be considered fortunate in connection with so great a calamity —the vessel lies in comparatively shallow water, and in such a position as to allow a very thorough examination to be made of every part. This in- vestigation cannot fail to dispose of the doub.t which at present exists as to the cause of the acci- dent. It is to be lipped the result will also be such as will remove the prevalent misgivings as te the seaworthiness of these speedy but fragile vessels.

The figures of the steamship companies carry- ing passengers between the United States and Great Britain are eloquent testimony as to the benefits which we derive from our American cousins. Six steamships which left New York the other day carried altogether only two hundred saloon passengers. Three steamships arriving in New York on the same day delivered back t° America 2,000 cabin passengers who had been spending part of their vacation with us. Of course the Americans cross the Atlantic to see Europe, and not merely from fondness for the island which gave them their speech and their guidance in literaturP. But still, wherever else they go, they never miss England or Scotland. The pilgrimages to Shakespeare's country acd to the home of the great "Sir Walter" are mostly made by Americans. TheY • heighten the value of all our old boeko, pictures and furniture, and they make just the difference between prosperity and a struggle to keep going for the proprietors of many hotels. They do not help the jewellery trade much, as they like to gat their valuables in that line and bijouterie in Paris; but in countless small ways this army of people with money to spend help our commerce. ThB number of British who go to America for a boll' day is surprisingly small. It would be better of us if more studied at first hand the enterprise an° skill of the American manufacturers. The British workman is still the most conscientious workins° in the world—the man who pre-eminently gives ll' finish to what he does, but he is not sufficientli alert and adaptable.

A Provincial Letter.

The matter is likely to be soon broughs to a bead so far as•the county borough of Cardiff le concerned, and when this has been done other sie3i- lar authorities may be expected to follow. A rasp!' ber of the Corporation in question has given notier: of a proposal which should the Council adopt will have the following effect. Aldermen at end of their term of six years will have to go the electors, and will then, if elected enter Council as councillors, and seek the suffrages that body co secure a return to the aldermaril,.; bench. There are various points to be considers. in connection with such proceeding. For install; how the sitting members in the different will like the idea of standing aside for, or enterl into competition with, the aldermen who 120,13„ thus be obliged to appeal to the electors. Tue,:j the system would assuredly have its sffect 1113`,',0 the multiplicity of elections, since, should I ti't ox retiring alderman pass through the double ordse : the:electorate and the Council, bye-elections vrth'es... result in order to fill up the vacancies in the, ;RI pective wards. relaxation of prison discipline,but rather to the pirit ot imitation which is eo often discernible in the world of criminality. The public are officially 'told that the idea of escaping seems to have seized the imagination of prisoners all over the provinces, and led to plots and contrivances which have rival- in their ingenuity the legendary escapades of lhe notorious criminals of fiction. Once again we are reminded that `• truth is stranger than fiction:' it has been so in some of these cases; but the Curious point is how a knowledge of the recent 4,ensational escapes found its way to the convicts Incarcerated in the various prisons. In theory, at ieast these people are segregated,and special efforts . 8 made to prevent intercommunication. But in spite of these safeguards news of special interest al:Tears to "circulate" within the prison walls,thus ffording another proof not only that Solid walls do not a prison make,

Or iron bars a cage under all circumstances, but that there is some fairly rich language of signs common to all members of he criminal fraternity.

An important Conference of Friendly Societies commences in London on Monday, and is to extend over three days. It is convened at the instance of the Hearts of Oak Yearly Dividing Friendly Societies, and will be by no means sectional. The invitation has been extended to all the chief or- ganisations, and it is expected that delegives from 411 these will be present to discuss the Old Age yensions question and the now historic Birming- ham speech of Mr Joseph Chamberlain. Since the latter declined to promote any scheme of the kind in a Government measure, it naturally became the duty of the friendly societies to take the matter in band. There has already been considerable dis- cussion among representatives of these societies as to the expediency of dt once formulating a scheme, no well as regarding the broad principles and de- rails of any proposition that might be submitted to Parliament in the form of a bill. But the discus- 8, ions that have taken place cannot be said to have led to any definition of polimand some prominent Oddfellows at leaqt appear to entertain the opinion that the demands created by the South African war have greatly interfered with the prospects of a State-aid scheme of old ago pensions. This, how- aver, does not imply that the subject is to be allowed to fall int 3 abeyance, and the Conference, it is hoped, may now arrive at an agreement as to the policy to be pursued. Still it has always to be remembered that there is a wide difference between the definition of a basis and the initiation of rarliamentary action, to say nothing of the gain- 'R of Parliamentary sanction to a further charge O a the Imperial exchequer.

Pity the sorrows of the Manchester Licensing Justices I Drink in one form or another, like the epirit of evil, the South African war, or taxation, %seine ever with us. Occasionally the licensing authorities of provincial towns and cities have ,been taxed by the press with evincing undue _leniency to publicans and brewers, and hence the Alanchester licensing justices have been constrained 'to say that they at least are not the greatest sinners in this respect They tell us that they themselves ,view with the greatest alarm the rapid increase of ,Icences in their midst, that the law actually com- pels them to grant licences which they in their discretion would absolutely refuse that they have m , authority over beerhouses, which contribute so largely to the sum total of prevalent drunkenness 'Or thwe licences granted by the excise without Irtferonce to, or consultation with, the licensing 131agistrates. They say, moreover, that the linens- -1.4g of spirit and wine dealers by the excise should la their opinion cease, and that the holders of Arocers' licences should take their chance of a grant 2r ref seal as is the case with mher licence holders This is certainly a very sound, equitable, and wise 'pronouncement. The Manchester licensing justices, Moreover, state that no fewer than 2,923 houses in sthe city are now licensed or one licence for every IS3OI the entire population. It is a startling state- ent and • one for others besides teetotalers and temperance reformers to ponder over The pre- Gent SA stern of licensing appears to be not so Much for the perpose of regulating as fur pro- moting the traffic in drink, but whether looked 111". in one way or the other, it is manifewly a eocial curse and a p.)litical folly.

" A Gardener," writing to the papers, wants to ,Jw what is to be dons in consequence of the terrible increase of birds "which has taken place .hthe country,since our County Councillors in their wisdom have become so assiduous in protecting the Winged tribe. He recommends that instead of their Publishing lists showing what birds are protected, w_hich lists few people care to wade through, or if they do cannot be expected to wholly remember, -twn_e authorities should issue notices setting forth hat kinds of eggs and of birds should be taken and destroyed. There are known to be many gardeners besides this solitary correspondent, in all parts of the provinces, who would bear out the Writers complaint, and no doubt support his sue- g,estinn. Both the number and the voracity of birds have been much commented upon during the past "a": 11. Popular sympathy, as a rule, is in favour _nf.the general protection of bird life,and one Is cer- T.uin.ly indisposed to Fay anything that may appear ngainst it, but there are admittedly two sides this as to almost every other question. 'those are evidently birds: and birds, and kinds that are the worst enemies of the fruit grower and farmer may possibly have been over- protected. Apart from the unusually destructive attacks on certain kinds of fruit this year, it would „,,,uicncet appear that birds have not only been more 'nnierous and greedy, but that they have become :lore 6. knowing," since it has been observed in cer- tain Places that they have developed a faculty for actually discriminating between the best flavoured 'Nit and that of an inferior quality !

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

POVERTY AT RUNCORN,

At the Runcorn Police Court on Monday about half-a-dozen people were excused payment of the poor rate on tne ground of poverty. add Lancashire Rugby combin FRODSHAM PETTY SESSIONS. At the Frodsham Petty Sessions on Wednesday before Mr J Ockleston, William Booth. and Arthur Fletcher were sent to gaol for three months hard labour for stealing a spring balance valued at 14s the property of William Horton. Superintendent Neild said they Were two of the worst men in the village and the chairman remarked that they were fortunate in not being sent to the quarter sessions.

A NEW RUNCORN COMP-ANY.

The Pwllheli Granite Company, Limited, regis- tered on September 11th, with a capital of £6,000, in £lO shares, to take over the business of quarry proprietors and stone merchants carried on by James and John Littler at Pwllheli and Minflordd, N. Wales,and at Runcorn has been formed. There will be no initial public issue. The first directors are J Littler and J Bebbington, the former being permanent director and chairman ; qualification 10 shares; remuneration as fixed by the company.

A PAWNBROKER'S SHOP ON FIRE. About six o'clock on Tuesday morning a large volume of smoke was seen issuing from the shop of Mr W Craddock, pawnbroker, Church-street, Run- corn, and an alarm was raised. Mr H Dodd, a painter employed in the shop of Mr Poynter. on the opposite side of the street, burst open the door. It was then seen that a considerable quantity of clothing was on fire. Buckets of water were thrown on the burning material with such effectiveness Ithat when the fire brigade arrived the flames were extinguished. It is supposed that a gas jet left burning all night had ignited some clothing. Abou .C2O damage has been done which is covered by in surance. P C Wilding rendPrad valuable assi-tsnea DAMAGING GROWING POTATOES. At the Runcorn Police Station on Saturday, before Mr E Imison, John Cowley. W Jameson, and P Holland, working on a dredger lying at Weston Point, were charged with damaging growing potatoes to the value of ss, the property of T Harti- son.—.P.C. Rowlands said that on the 20,h inst he was passing along the prosecutor's field when he saw the defendants carrying two bundles. He exatnined them and found they contained p3tstoeB which had been pulled up from a field close to where they were standing.—Prosecutor said that stealing potatoes from his field had been goin¢ on for some time.—Defendants were fined 23 6.1 and 5s costs and the amount of the damage, or in default 7 days. INTERESTING PRESENTATION AT RUNCORN. On Friday evening, the teachers connected with the Holy Trinity Church Sunday 6chools, including the Rev C H Killick (vicar), Rev RJ Poole (curate), Mr J Woods (superintendent) and Mr A E Hulse (secretary), met at the Mariners' Church, for the purpose of presenting Mr J Killeross with a beauti- ful dinner service, on the occasion of his marriage. An excellent ,tea was provided, which was much enjoyed. After tea Mr Woods spoke of the useful services Mr Killcross bad rendered as a teacher in the Sunday school, and of the true spirit in which he had worked. Mr Killcross responded. A good programme of music made the rest of the evening very pleasant: INTERESTING WEDDING AT RUNCORN.— DUNCALF—

LITTLER.

A very pretty wedding took place on Wednesday at St Paul's Chapel, the contracting parties being Miss Sarah Ellen Littler, daughter ot Mr William Littler, Leincester Gardens, to Frederick Dancalf, son of Mr James Duncalf, Fox-street, Runcorn. Both parties are exceedingly well known being closely identified with Camden Chapel and Sunday school. The Rev A Wells, superintendent minister, officiated. The bride was given away. by her brother Mr 0 W Littler. her wedding gown was composed of heliotrope fabric trimmed with crape de chine and silk embroidery: She also wore a crino- line hat trimmed with chiffon plumes, and orAnge blossom and carried a splendid bouquet of lilies and crysanthemums. The bridesmaids were the Misses B Littler, sister, and Miss Duncalf, the sister of the bridegroom. They wore dresses of plum coloured cloth trimmed with scarlet, with hats to match. They carried bouquets of mar- .guerites. The best man was Mr GII Marsh and the groomsman was Mr W Littler. The church was crowded, and after the ceremony a reception was held at the hums of the bride. The honeymoon will be spent at Llandulas.

RUNCORN W ESLEYAN CIRCUIT QUA.RTERLY

3iEETING.

The above quarterly meeting was held on Mon- day for the first time in its history at Camden, it having previously been held at Brunswick, There was a very large attendance every place save Preston Brook being well represented. Tea was provided by Mr and Mrs S C Beckett and at the close of the tea a very hearty vote of thanks was given to them for their hospitality. The business meeting followed immediately after,the chair being occupied by the Rev Alfred Wells, superintendent minister of the circuit. He was supported by his colleagues the Revs H Morrison and B Foster and the circuit stewards. The new secretary, Mr J Povah was warmly welcomed on taking his seat, The financial stwernent presented by the stewards was of a highly gratifiying character The . total income amounted to £212 17s 51 and the evpenses to; £173 13s, leaving a bal- ance in hand to the credit of the circuit of £39 44 sd. The numerical statement pre- sented by the chairman showed that notwithstand- ing the heavy loss by deaths and removals the circuit still held its own the numbers being 742 full members; 84 on trial, and 229 in junior society classes. Mr A R Norman, JP, was appointed cir- cuit treasurer to the aged ministers fund. A com- mittee for foreign mission was re-appointed, the treasurer being Mr J R Frith and the secretaries the Rev H Morrison and Messrs E Taylor and E Knive- ton. Mr Thomas Cook was appointed temperance sectetary for the circuit in place of the late Mr J D Jones. The Rev J B Forster was also associated with him in the office. At the'request of the chair- man the meeting gave permission for the purchase of land at Halton for the enlargement of the Sunday school. It was announced that the Gospel Car would shortly visit Weston Point and Preston Brook. It was arranged for a Convention of workers engaged in different depart- ments of Church work of the circuit ta be held as soon ao convenient, arrangements being left in the hands of the Ministers. Mr Povah gave a very clear and comprehensive statement showing the position of the" Twentieth Century Fund" of the Circuit. The statement revealed the fact that more than £f,400 had been paid, in and that about £lOO more was required to complete the amount pledg- ed by the circuit, namely the sum of sixteen hun- dred guineas. A short discussion followed, the meeting pledging itselQto do its utmost to secure the completion of the, fund by the Christmas quarterly meeting to be held on the 16th December.
