ME EXAMINER FRIDAY SEPTEMBER 27 1901.

RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT.] FOOTBALL.

There was a very fine gate at Irwell-lane on Saturday to witness what proved to be an interest— ing struggle between two of the crack clubs of Cheshire and Yorkshire. Both towns sent their strongest representative team, and the struggle was as keen as could he expected between two such teams- The 'Linnets," however, fully justified their reputation, and:Halifax have to mourn not only the bitterness of a crushing defeat but are also denied the consolation of even managing to pierce the home defence. The game can truly be said to have been one of the finest played at Runcorn for eome time. Despite the strong feeling between the partizans of the respective teams there was an utter absence of anything approaching brutal play on the part of the players. At times the combination of each was all but perfect, anl the attack and defence were as pretty and as scientific as one could wish to see. Perhaps there was a little too mach of the kick, rush, and mark game but on the whole the ball was kept loose.

In the earlier part of the game it must be in all fairness conceded that the visisors rather out- classed the home contingent and indeed pressed them very severely so that Runcorn were in a sense fortunate in gaining a lead qf three points before half-time. The play which led to Jones crossing the line was very smart work indeed, and, in fact, the other points which were secured at the expense of Halifax were the result of well judged combina- tion and were not at all attributable to slices of luck. In the latter half things went decidedly easy for Runcorn, and the visitors had not a look in at all being forced to defend practically the whole of the time. The scrums gave the leather to Jolley and Faulkner over and over again, and but for smart tackling Runcorn would have piled up a much larger score than they actually did.

At half back Halifax were simply outplayed, and though the threequarter line of the visitors got away once or twice they were not allowed to take any liberties whici might have made them danger- ous. Little made a ridiculously pour show against Houghton, who played a dashing game from the commencement. Walker led the front rank on in fine style and in the rear division it would be hard to single out a weak spot unless it was lnglesfield, who, at times, works but half heartedly. The day's performance reflects nothing but credit on each individual player and there may well be re- joicing in the Runcorn camp.

There was but little business to be transacted at the Runcorn Rural District C3uncil on Tuesday. There were, however, two interesting matters which afforded a very neat little discussion. Both arose out of that most übiquitous odour at Thel wall which comes and goes in the most mysterious manner possible. Judging by the letters which have been received during the last fortnight by Mr Ashton (clerk), the Committee who thought to remedy the serious state of affairs by cleaning out the ditch, and ignoring any supposed evil at Messrs Long's Tannery, have incurred the id-will of the inhabitants, and have come in for a good sound rating, by letter ot course, for their pains. The Committee who openly expressed tneir opinion that the root of the evil did not lay in Messrs. Long's tanyard have caused, to use a mild term, absolute consternation in the minds of the residents from Messrs. Naylor and Reynolds to some humble individual who evidently finds it difficult to pen a letter but who is goaded into a 'floe frenzy" of writing by the nonchalance of those who ought to protect the bast interests of the people at Thelwatl.

Mr. Wallington thought the letters which bad been sent ought to be read and with a certain amount of heat tried to carry his point He was however a trifle mollified when the clerk said that the letters were all to the effect, that the mere cleaning of the ditch was not sufficient, and the nuisance still existed. Tae clerk further pointed out that the matter was now under the considera- tion of the Local Government Board and that the correspondence, together with the Surveyor's report on the houses situate in Weaste-lane would be forwarded in their entirety for them to deal with. Taus the vexed question lies and what will ultimately be done pas.,es the wit of man to conceive. One thing ie quite certain, the Council as a body are sick and ire d of the whole affair and would, it is veritably believed consign cor- respondence, agitators and reports to Limbo if they dared.

-- There is only one person who has reason to be pleased and proud of himself on account of the affair and he is the expert Dr Penny. On Tuesday he again sent his small bin of sixty five guineas to the Council for payment, and, wisely or unaviqdy, they refused to pay it immediately. It will be remembered that Dr Adams, the medical offi3er,and Dr Penny's reports are entirely at variance and the Council believe that they are only acting fairly towards theruselve4, and the ratepayers, by asking for the critic'siii of Dr Penny on the strictures of Dr Adams. The Clerk explained that he had already sent the report of the medical officer o the expert, but he had received no reply from him and he did not feel justitied in asking him to make a report lest the Council should incur.addition- al expense. Thus the decision arrived at practically was that as soon as Dr Penny makes an additional report--on the' for nothing" principle—his account will be paid, which is rather a one-sided way of looking at things.

TYPolice Courts abound in humour, pathos and tragedy. At the Runcorn Court the otaer day a tale was told of blighted happiness caused by the'myrinidons of the law which a skilled romancer could weave into an interesting story. A tall gaunt and hagaard woman was brought in the dock on a charge of having assaulted one of the most gallant sergeants in the Runcorn force. Having occasion to call at the house of the prisoner to transact a little businese of a private nature with her brother, a notorious charactier, the female became so exas- perated at the unwarranted intrusion of the worthy officer that she had little compunction in enforc- ing her a-gunients with a liberal dose lof the poker. The prisoner's brother and mother also happening to be hard by grasped the situation at once, and the official too, so that among the three he had a lively and per- haps interesting time. Fortunately a comrade in arms heard the parley-cons "in the house and making his way inside rescued his mots unfortu- nate brother. Of course there was nothing for it but to take the woman into custody who had been 1 the cause of the disaster, and as the " tete-a-tete '! happened on a Saturday she was consigned to the cells to await her trial on the Monday morning: By a strange fatality Ole Sunday should have been her wedding day and instead of hearing the sound of .wedding bells on the morning of her nuptials she heard the clAnk of the warders keys and the tramp of the jailer. Obvionvly her would-be spouse could not summon up suffi-ient courage to bail her out fearing lest a worse evil might tefall him than the mere bending of a poker across his back. It has not since transpired whether he still intends to lead his blushing bride to Hymen's altar but the magis- trates were convinced that her ardent affections had received such a rude shock that further punish- ment was unnecessary. Our readers will be pleased to know that she was simply bound over to keep the peace.

The tastelfor music appears to be on the incregse I in Runcorn, which is a very good sign indeed. The Runcorn male voice choir is going strong, as a sportsman would say, and the Runcorn Orchestral Society is again making a bold bid for something more than mere existence. Every intelligent per- son must regret the lack of support which made the !foundations crumble of the" Tonic-Soil a," " Musical," and the " Philharmonic " Societies. At present there is only the " Orchestral " which caters for the public taste, and if tbe only remain- ing musical institution is to be bound up in the ! warp and woof of the town it is essential that during the coming winter they shall receive that support from the public which is vital to financial success. Musical critics who heard the band last season asstrt that there is not a flaw in any of the parts, and the musical treats which are given to the public are pleasant in their tone and stimulat- ing in their effects. "success and prosperity " may safely be wished to the organization.

--- The lecture by the,Rev J Flannagan on "A walk round my parish" was one of the most interesting given in Runcorn for many months. The rev gentleman is truly one of tbe most remarkable personalities of tiny present day. Be stands fully six feet high, is somewhat thin and spare, is ener- getic, and has the Irish element stampAd on every action. Re has an almost magnetic influence over his hearers and by turns has them in bursts

of laughter or sobs of pity. He has a wonderful fund of aneclotes from real life which are strung together in his lectures by flashes of genius which not only delight the cultured but appear irresistably to the unlearned. His long, white, nervous hands are one of the most peculiar features connected with him. They are on the work the whole of the time he lectures and weave a wonderful charm, as it were, over his audi- ence. Though there is a broad rollicking humour in everything he says there is an under current \of sadness which tells that the iron of bitterness has, at one time eaten down vary deeply in his soul Perhaps his early youth may account for it or per- haps the sad sights which he sees in his slum work. Atter all he is a veritable John the Baptist for he has a message to deliver, and his is a voice crying from the slums for the people of England to make straight the paths of the oppressed and suffering

Is is stated on good authority that before very long a new works, in connection with Mathieson's will be erected near Rock Savage. At pre3ent the officials who are interested in the scheme refuse to give any information respecting the plans, etc., but negotiations are in progress, and there is every reason to believe there is something more in the statement than mere rumour. I heard a very inter- estinz bit of gossip the other day from one likely to know wnich may °rimy of may not prove to be correct. For some two years a gentleman residing near Liverpool has been engaged in experimenting with copper in the hope of being able to extract therefrom some of the more precious metals. About 6 months ago he came to the neighbourhood of Run- corn to develop a certain line of discovery which he had made. Proving successful in his researches a company has been approached with a view of securing the patent, anti it is said that should the negotiations prove successful that Runcorn will be the site of the companys works. It is devoutly to be wished that both these " storiis of new works " will prove correct. What with the transporter and all toe improvements which the Local Authority have in hand not forgetting the cry of "down with the rates," it will go far towards making this once " Merrie Runcorn '' of ours a veritable little gold mine for business men and working people !!

VISIT OF THE REV. J. FLANNAGAN TO RUNCORN.

On Wednesday in connection with the:Prinoitive Methodist Chapel, the Rev J Flannegan of the London Mission preached a very instructive sermon on texts from Mark avi,l3 and Lake xxiv, 18. The subject was spiritual visions and the preacher deelt with the purifie cation of heart lea ling to spiritual visions. In the evening the rev gentleman gave a meet interesting lecture on" A walk round my Parish." There were very large congregations at both services. Mr Taylor presided in the evening and said his duty was a very light one. It would be one of the biggest mistakes he bed ever made if he were to attempt to make a speech. Serne chair- men spoiled the; lecture to which the audimce looked forward to. He was delighted to b 3 with them, and he was disappointed that he could not be with them last year. He bad heard such a lot of Mr Flannagan that he had determined to be with them and hear the lecture From the title they might look forward with pleasure and interest to it. (Applause.) The Rev J le,annagan, who was received with applause, said that be was glad that the devil had no hand in the making of him. It was a pleasing theurtit for one t remember that they were God made men and women in a God made world. The devil ceuld not make a man. He Could not even turn stones lute bread. He once tried and failed. But the Devil could spoil God-made men and he could I blight a God-made world. He could lay desolate many a fair epee that ought to bloom as the garden of Eden I Man had had placed within him an innate sense of the beautiful and an instinceive love of the sublime and shrunk from all things repulsive. The visitor to London wanted to see the bright part of life, the latest fashior and styles, yet he did not know but it were as fair foe them, and perhaps more in the divine order,if they drew aside the silk curtains through which they looked upon me so that they might know something of the tragedy behind it. Some time aeo he went to Belfast and a gentleman wished to show bine the file buildioga of the city, but us (the speaker) said to him " Lake me to the slums." Tat was his philosophy. it might he that of s lunatic, but he believed it was the philosophy of God to judge the character of a people in the front arreet by the way people were livio in the back street. He did net care for the civilization that coeld make the superstructure in the front magnificent but which left men and women—God made and God redeemed to rot in misery, shams and degradation. (Applause.) A man might carry the wisdom of the city in his skull but if the man's feet were diseased then the body would rest upon an insecure and shake basis. And the body of a state rested upon its feet and if they were diseased or unclean it was insecure no matter how magnificent the front might be. Thousands of people were degraded in their maenificent English constitution and were rotting in misery and many of them were starving in Go Pa England. That kind of thing could not go on much longer, and the trireme itself rested upon an insecure basis The only way to make it secure was to treat the • people a little better than the London cab horses and ladies' pet poodles. All the bloodshed would be remem- bered in Heaven and there would be a smash up some i day as sure as Gad reigned. If politicians pursued the law of expediency in cent% adiction to the law of righteousness God would bring the whole horrible conce n down to the ground, and the sooner the better. Ignorance, poverty, and slum-don), were the three things on which anarchy rested. When President McKinley's assassin replied, "I did I not shoot ihe man," it was false philosophy. but the man's idea of the things was that he shot the principle, not the man. The bullet going through the man did not kill the principle. Principles could only be killed by Principles ; unreeson by reason ; wrong by right. Remove the cause and the effect would cease, and there would be no more assassinations of Presidents, Kings and Queens. Linden, however, was getting better every day. They could take it from a man who went through the worst cases of poverty next to Charles Booth, He lead been 10 years working in the slums and he did not think he was too optimistic in his tone when he said his disttict was getting better. He believed the time would come when they weuld hunt London streets for a hungry child and would not find one and would then superannuate the workhouse. But it would only be when the gin shone were gone. Oid London bridge, where more than one unfortunate had gone to llaer death Was the boundary line of his parish. The waters with their inky blackness seemed to quiver with sympathy. Charles Dickens loved his district and from London Bridge to the old church he often wandered in search of characters. When wanting a sneaking Jewish Villain he went to Whitechapel but he get his Bill Sykes frcrn this district, and he used to walk the streets at 12 o'clock at night to see phases of life he could not See anywhere else. Lots of children in his district never knew what chielhoed was. As Kingsley said they were " damned " in the world though he would not say they were damned out of it. All the shadows in their density darkened the life of the child who leapt at once from childhood into old age. Some he had seen were beautiful with classic caste which told of high blood in their veins. And he had seen them change gradually until all that was keen, and crafty seem to be impressed on the flesh of their countenance. But his heart went out to them and to all who despised the parson, arid he tried to get hold of them. They were rough diamonds out of which God could bring splendid lives by his grace. To win a man was more to him than to win Waterloo and he would rather lead a soul to Jesus Christ than have the honours given to the mighty Wellington. John Bunysn came his district as a local preacher and was followed by ladies and gentlemen who preferred his eloquence to that of the Bishop of London. Cruden,toe who gave them the Bible Concordance, rests with them. Somewhere underneath vats of beer in a large brewery his bones lay though no one could determine the exact snot. Oliver Goldsmith, who wrote the English classic, the "Vicar of Wakefield, " made his pills and plasters on the Old Bank Side. Out of his district had Pone literatu that had enriched the workP and men who bad laid the foundations of the United States of America. .Shakes- Peare lived in his district and Wrote his flays that were played at the Globe Theatre. Plays with a true and lofty meaning against the vile dramas that had laid hold of the public taste. Shakespeare and his brother Edward died in tie dis- trict so poor that there was no money with which to bury them. Another name was that of Philip Massinger one of the first of English poets who filled his throat with nne clear note of music and then starved to death. Some kind friends took the bones lay and put them in a grave and on the stone engraved " Philip Masainger, a stranger." Colonel Barnaby had confessed his indebted- ness to the influence which he had received while listen- ing to his ministry. He, the preacher was born in Mansfield, Nottingham, and it was in that town he found redemption and he then went to preach to the men who had known him. For four years he had toe largest congregation outside London. The membership was 2,800, and the Confer- ence took him out of that magnificent place and put him in a dirty chapel in London. From haviag a con- gregation of 11 on the first Sunday morning, he rose it until at the evening service he had two to three thou- sand. He had the pleasure of purchasing the site of tbe public-house he saw on the first Sunday morning, acid on which was now erected a sanctuary. Two churches bad already despaired of reaching the masses when he got there, and had retired, but he got in touch with the men through their wives. He would never forget his first Sunday and even now

when 10 years bad elapsed be was reminded of it. There was not a m fal in the district would hurt a hair of his head and when he put up for the Board of Guardians he was returned without having canvassed a single vote. They had between 600 and 700 children in the Sunday School who had been got out of the streets. He intended to work on at St George's Hall and he asked f )r the co-operation of all present. The lecture was interspersed throughout with pleasant anecdotes and was one of the finestdelivered in Runcorn for many months. _ . . . . _ . . .

A vote of thanks to the lecturer and the chairman was given on the proposition of the Rev W Eyre. At the tea meeting in the afternoon the following ladies presided at the tables Mesdames James Stubbs, Jab( z Stubbs, R Stubbs, Eyre Thomason. kfardley. Jackson, Withingtoniand the Mims Lunt, A Fleet, and Eyre.

HARVEST FESTIVALS.

St. Barnabas' Church.

The harvest thanksgiving services were continued in the above church on Sunday. At 8 ain there was a celebration of the Holy Communion, the numbsr of com- municants Leing very satisfactory. Matins and choral Communion followed at 10 30, when the preacher was the Rev F E Powell, Vicar of & James', Latchford. There was a good congregation. The preacher based his sermon on the words " Behold, the days come. saith the Lord God, that I will send a famine; in the land, not a famine of bread, nor a thirst for water, but of hearing the words of the Lord." He spoke of the indifference to religion which prevailed at the present time, and said that the most terrible calamity that could befal a nation was a religious famine—a famine of hearing the words of the Lord.— In the afternoon a children's service was held, when the vicar (Rev J H G Hollingworth) delivered an address. There was a very large congregation at the evening ser- vice, not a seat being unoccupied. The preacher was the Rev A Goodall, of Eccles, who selected for his text " Whatsoever a man soweth that shill he also reap." Cialatians xi., xvii., and urged his hearers to make the best use of God's blessings, Special harvest hymns were sum:, the 'choir being assisted by a number of instrumentaliets.—The collections at all tho services were on behalf of the choir fund.

Legh-street Primitive Methodist.

Oa Sunday large congregations assembled at Legh- street for the Harvest Festival. The chapel was decorated for the occasion with considerable taste and originality. There was no scarcity of fruit and other produce for the decorators to Exercise their ingenuity upon, and every- thing was used to the fullest advantage. If one part of the decorations was more striking than another, it was an anchor' formed with rice with a backround of peas and Indian corn. Special sermons were preached morn- ing and evening by Mr East. of Alexandra College, Manchester, and in the afternoon a sacred cantata was given by the choir, entitled " Harvest Home in the Temple Days." The soloists on this occasion were Miss Bridge, Miss E Miss D Jackson, and Messrs Corrin, Bissell, Osborne and Greenough. 'Toe caatata contains some very fine work for soloists and chorus, besides extremely pretty and effective pieces for male and female voices separately. Perhaps it is not out of place to remark that great improvement is noticeable in the choir, more parti- cularly in the male voices. This improvement is greatly to the credit of the conductor, Mr A Spruce, who is to be congratulated on the reputation his choir is fast gaining in the town and neighbourhood. At the morn- ing service the anthem " All Thy work; praise Thee " (Smallwood) was given,and in the evening the following pieces were rendered r—S3lO and chorus, "Hear my prayer " (Mendelssohn) ; solo, " Eternal Rest," (Piccolomini) Mr J Carrin ; and chorus, " Achieved is Thy glorious work," (Haydn). The highest praise is to be bestowed upon Miss Bridge for her rendering of the solo in Mendelssohn's beautiful anthem, and upon the choir for the support they gave her in the chorus work Altogether the anthem " Hear my prayer " was a distinct musical treat. Toe very decided success of the ch,ral work was in no small measure due to the able organist, Mr F Podmore, who played the accompaniments with taste and intelligence. —On Monday evening a fruit barquot was held, and the Rev Charles Finch took the chair, in the absence of Mr J r Rowland, who was presented from being present by a severe cold. An interesting programme was gone through. Miss Bridge sang a very sweet song, and Miss Eva Miller was hoard to advantage in " Midst the hush of the: Golden Corn." Mr Greenough delighted the audience with the tenor solo " Let me like a Soldier fall " ; and Mr Albert Spruce sang with great spirit Jude's fine song " The Skipper." Mr Corrin sang the ever welcome song " Tom Bowling " ; Mr Sam Rowland played a violin solo "The Broken Melody " with great feeling and ex- pression, and Mr Whitfield gave a recitation enti.led "Jugged Rare," for which the audience demanded an encore, end he responded with " While the Sabbath bells are ringing " accompankd by his son on the auto harp. Master Whitfield also gave two selections on the auto harp. The Legh-street Male Voice Choir, sang two part songs " Sleep my lady sleep." and " Comrades in arms," for which they were twice recalled. All present joined in singing the Doxology, thus bringing to a close one of the most successful harvest festivals ever held in this place of worthip. Mr Fred Podmore was the acconananist. It is pleasing to notn that the financial success is proportionate to the 811C,IPSS achieved in other directions, for the proceeds are in advance of former years.

Primitive Methodist Chapel, Runcorn.

On Sunday was held the annual festival in connection with the above place of worship. The ladies had be- stowed considerab'e care on the arrangement of the flowers, fruit, and foliage, and the sacred edifice pre- sented quite a charming appearance. The pillars had been very neatly draped with long strings of coloured flowers and leaves, while be- tween them hung baskets which held a collection of the most lovely ferns, dahlias, and asters imaginable. The conimunion rails were decorated with a fringe of oats and leaves intermingled with flowers, while the cushion at the foot held a splendid collection of some of the choicest fruits of the earth. The table held a most faithful representation in bread of a wheat- sheaf and on either side were ranged agricultural imple- ments. Within the rails was also a very small picture- esque stack of wheat which gave a pretty appearance to the view. The choir stalls had been very neatly and effectively decorated by the choir who did their work in such a manner as to leave nothing to be desired. A large harp composed of oats, flowers and foliage and strung with strings of small red crab apples fronted the organ, while on either side were festooned lengths of pretty creepers from which hung asparagus fern. The pulpit was a very pretty work of art. The centre panel contained the motto of the harvest " Plenty," worked with red crab on a light background, and fringed on either side with fruits of the harvest, the window sills, top, had come in for a good share of attention and literally groaned beneath a profusion of fruit. The prettiest decoration, however, was a vase covered with violet asters and daintily relieved • with white stocks. In the morning tho preacher was

the Rev .113 Foster,who delivered an excellent discourse on the value of giving thanks for our many blessings. In the evening there wag a large congregation, the chapel being crowded to its utmost capacity. The choir in the rendering of each showed uniform excellence, and showed unmistakable signs of careful rehearsal. The Solos were given with equal credit by Messrs T Midge. A Shingler, and T Williams. The collections towards the same fund were also satisfactory.

A Lymm Licensing Application Refused. At the Altriucham Licensing Sessions on Monday James Beswick, of the Crown Hotel beerhouse, Booth's Hill, Lymm, applied for a wine on-licence. The police opposed, as d.d Mr Ridgway, solicitor of Warrington. on behalf of a number of property owners. Mr Becton, solicitor, in presenting the applicacion,said the applicant was constantly asked for wine by visitors to the house, and he submitted. that the req lest of Mr Beswick that he should be granted a wine licence Vqe3 a reasonable one. Ap— plicant, in his evidence, said he did not cater tor picnic parties on Sundays, and in cross-examina— tion said that whatever rowdyism there might have been in Lymm lately there had been no rowdv, characters supplied at his house. Mr Ridgway said he had nothing whatever to say againsi.• applicant's house, but he thought a wine licence was not needed for the premises. A similar application was made last year, and was refused, and he contended that no new circumstances had arisen such as would warrant the granting of the licence aßked for now. The Bench took this view, and refused the applica— tion.

F. OMETHING ABOUT THE AUTHOR.

MR. WALTER WOOD first became well-known as a writer of stories dealing with military life through the medium of the London magazines.whose editors were not slow to recognise the unusually close acquaintance with barrack and campaign life and the dry humour which his work consistently re— vealed. He possesses the fundamental quality 119Ced- wiry to success as a novelist—the ability to tell a story ; and the reader's interest never flags.

Of his first published volurne,sof military stories the London "Daily Telegraph " said, ''There are few stronger or more captivating stories in the English language." A companion volume of naval articles won the rare honour, for a young writer, of a special—and generous—article in " The Saturday Review." from that distinguished naval critic, the late Admiral Colomb.

EOMETHING ABOUT THE STONY.

A QUEEN'S SOLDIER "is a story of Yorkshire and of the South African battlefields. Its hero is a trooper in the Impsrial Yeomanry, who leaves his father's farm and his sweetheart for the dusty veldt and the interminable hardships of the campaign. The author's experience in war subjects enables him, without apparent effort, to bring before the reader a brilliant series of pictures of like in the field. While he offers plenty of excitement in the way of skirmishing and of more serious battles, he never loses sight of the obligation as a novelist to give a human interest to his work, and the events of the war are shown, not as matters of dull history, hut as they appeared to Mark Berm, trooper in the Imperial Yeornanry,while performing his duty as a soldier of the Queen. Bean is wounded in a skirmish, and is taken as a prisoner to Pretoria. Events enable him at length to escape, but only to undergo terrible sufferings. Perhaps this portion of the tale will be found to possess the most thrilling interest for readers. Benn's experiences in his wild clash for liberty are partly founded on fact and they are graphically described. There are afterwards some glimpses of life in hospital, and the tale ends as happily as everyone who rn. 'tea the acquaintance of Mark Benn and his svveeth, art woute desire it to do. "A Queen's Soldier," a. the work of one who knows how to write intelligently about war, with- out overloading the picture, will, we confidently believe, be found to rank among the best stories which have been written about the South African strnale.

ASIAULTED BY A LATCHFORD MAN. John Burns, le.ederick-street, Latchford, WA3 charged at Daiesbury Petty eessions on fuesday with assaulting PC. Williams:whilst in the execution of his duty on the Ist September, also with doing damage to a lamp. the property of the Cheshire County Council, to the extent of is 6d. D, fendant was represented by Mr T S Steel, and pleaded not guilty.—Before the hearing of the evidence, Mr Steel said that defendant had been charged with stealing nibs of potatoes on the same date. He understood that the Case had been with- drawn, and he applied for it to be marked "dis- missed." The magistrates complied with OA] request. P.C. Williams, stationed at Grappenhall, said that a., five minutes to twelve p.m., on Sunday. September Ist, be was in Chester-road, Grappenhall, near Limb Brook Bridge, when he met the defendant coming from the direction of Apolekton Thorn, accempanied by two grey- hounds. When defendant turned the corner witness observed that the man's pockets were very bulky. Wanes!' stopped him and said, " What have you got there ?" Defendant said "Nothing." Barns was then carrying a large puce of wood in his right hand. Wit- ness then searched the pockets, and found 121,b5. of potatoes. On asking where he got them from Burns replied, "From a man on the road from Appleton, th.t Ido not know him." Defendant gave the name of "Smith, Warrington." Witness said, "All light, Burns, a very handy name is Smith.'' He told defendant he would have to go with him to Stockton Heath Police Station. When about 20 yarda up the road Burns put his right foot out and tripped witness. He had his lamp with him and fell on it, damaging it to the extent of 19 61. His hand was also cu'. Defendant threw off his cost and ran in the direction of Latchford Without. Witness pursued him, but failed to catch him. At 12 a.m. the following morning P.C. Hardy and he went to defendant's house and apprehended him. VVhen charged Burns said he knew nothing about the matter —Cross- examined: It was a moonlight night. D fondant walked vietly along for 80 cards. D fendant tripped him and slipped his coat. Witness denied that the wood got between his legs and tripped him.— Mr. Ste 4 said that the defendant's story was that he was stepped by the constable when going under Lumb Brook Bridge, and when P.C. Wil iams asked him to go he said " All right, I will go." He walked away about 80 yards and then defendant foolishly tried to get out ot the constable's grasp. He slipped his coat off the left arm, when the piece of wood got between the con- stable'a legs and tripped him up. D .fendant was a respectable young man and bad been working at Messrs Fletcher Russells and Company's works for a number of yeaxs. In consequence of having been arrested he had lost his employer.ent.—Burns was then charged with stealing a ferret, the property of Mr J Charton Parr, at Grappenhall, on the 11th August.—Defendant was fined 104 and mils and for the theft 103 and costs (£2 14s in all.)

Williams said that on the date in question he was standing near his station when he saw Quinn driving a bay horse attached to a cart an Bradshaw's-lane, Grappsnhall. He turned the horse into the field. On examination he found that the animal was very lame, the near fore foot being very much swollen and Inflamed. In reply to a question, defendant sail his driver told him to take the horse out. He saw Clare twenty minutes later and he Bald the ' ho-se had been lame for a mo-ith or ea, and bad done no work during that period.—lnspector Print said that he examined the horse and found that this was a case of " chronic" lameness.—Clare said that the horse had a crushed foot and he thought it would do it good oa the field as the ground was soft.—He was ordered to pay ZOs and costs, and the costs of the summons azainst Quinn.

Warrington's 111-Luck at Broughton.

Warrington and Broughton Rangers met at Brough- ton on Saturday, and as each side had so far won all their engagements the interest in the encounter was very keen. The weather was damp, but notwithstand- ing the drizzle a crowd of about 9,000 assembled at VVheater's Field to watch the play. Warrtngton were fully reprerented,and the Rangers also had their selected team out though it was somewhat changed from the one which hsd done duty in previous matches The field was in brio order-which is rather unusual at Lower Broughton. Warrington : Flaßani, full back; Fish, Isherwood, Dickenson and Harris, three-quarters; Allen and Bur- gess, half-backs ; Morrison, Fdl, Edmoncson, Scholtze, Eden, Cunnineham, J Swift and F Swift, forwards.

S James came away with a tricky run but his pass to Widdeson went into touch, Allen punting into the home quarter. Dickenson improved matters with a smart run, but slow work behind the scrimmage by Burgess prevented the attack being successful. The forwards then rushed the ball to the line, Hoge clearing. Fish mulled a catch and the Rangers at once dashed into the Warrington half, a round of passing being again checked by Fish at the corner. Dickenson received from Allen and dodged past the home backs. but Thompson brought him down. A long punt by Wilson was splendidly followed on, and Hallam did well to save on the llie. Keen plan followed. the Rangers combining excellently and meeting a superb defence. Wilson and Graham made repeated efforts to get in, but Fish and Isner- wood never missed grassing the man with the ball. Warrington worked their way to centre by go forward play but were soon detending again, the Rangers b-ing, the more aggressive team. Widdesen made a fice effort at the corner but fell to a host I tacklers. Warrington were kept busy defending, but they gave the Rangers little latitude in the back divi- sion. Burgess was penalised and W James had an easy goal kick opposite the posts but failed to land a goal. Just before the interval Fish was carried off with an injured leg. When the whistle sounded Warrington had- got to mid-field again and there was no score. After the interval play was resumed by Thompson, Fish taking his usual place on the Warrington side though obviously lame. There was no goal and the sides were now equal again. Warrington lad the advantage of a slight breeze and showing plenty nit dash they got the upper hand of the Rangers again. Itherwond speedily folios. ed up and tackled Harry in his own quarter. Splendid football on both sides was now witnessed, Warrington getting the better of some exchange kicking and keep- ing the Rangers penned. Wilson and Graham ran to centre' but the relief was only temporary, Warrington quite overplaying their opriOnenta as time went On. Hogg was upset close to his line, and a penalty accruing to the visitors, Hallam kicked a file goal from long range. The score was signalised by the Rangers making a desperate rush to the Wartinetcn half, Fish falba:: a victim to numbers opposite the posts. Exciting play followed, the Warrington backs defending grandly. Slowly they drove the home team back, Harris inter- cepting a pass and running well into the home half. Warrington Spectators. Wattle Davies's Opinion of Them.

The Worst in England.

The following letter appeared in the " Yorkshire Evs wog Post "on Saturday, from Mr Wattle Davies,the well-known Batle7 footballer, whose app°arance at the Warrington Police Court on the charge of assaulting a sp(ctator, was reported in our last issue :—" I notice in your paper, " The York,hire Evening Post,"an account of the withdrawala the summons taken out by myself against a Warrington spectator, and also of the with- drawal of one by the spectator against myself. I should like to give Yorkshire people a proper idea as to what took place at Warrington on the 7th of this month. The game was a very keen one, as the Warringtoniana were evidently bent on taking their revenge tor their cup defeat in April of this year. Burgess, one of the Warrington half backs, and I chanced to incur the displeasure of the ref9-ee, with the result that we got sent off the field. As I walked down the touch-line I was struck by a spectator, and I then jumped over the rails and retaliated, but the police soon ioterfered,and very little cattle of it. To-day (Friday). I had to appear in answer to the summons, and I was . .

persuaded to apologize so that the matter might be hushed up in the interests of sport,with the result that, although I was the assaulted party, I have to practi- cally acknowledge that I was the guilty party. Instead or hushing things up, the Warrington C:mmittee have, despite the agreement we came to given the affair as much publicity as they possibly could. A friend of •mine in Warrington advised me, to COMO to some agree- ment instead ot taking the cats into court. As I had no witnesses myself—we (Batley) havinx no spectators with us—l thought the best thing I could do in the case was to, if possible, come to some terms, which I was persuaded to do to my sorrow. lam writicg this to warn all cluts in Yorkshire who are desirous cf taking care of their players, to keep a good guard over I them, as the Warrington spectators are the worst spectators in England.'

This match was contested on the litter's ground, and was watched by a good crowd. After an eventful game, in which neither team proved superior, they left the enclosure with a draw of five goals each. Runcorn Parish v. Richmond's, Warrington. These teams met in a Warrington and district League match at Runcorn on Saturday hrefore a fair number of spectators. Immediately after the start the visitors forced matters, and two goals were quickly placed to their credit. The Parish then smartened, and made an attack on the visitors' goal, but nothing came of it. Play was afterwards fairly even, the home team show- ing by far better combination than the visitors. During the second portion ot the game the visitors' goal was continually besieged, and two goals were the outcome of splendid combination. They gave the visitors no Chance to score, yet the latter defended sturdily, and the game ended in a draw of two goals each. Combination. Newton-le-Willows v. Tranmere Rovers,

Played at Newton on Saturday. Newton tried a new full back named Steele, who gave a very satisfactory exhibition. The visitors started and Newton immedi- ately tack up the running, a pretty centre from Billing- ton rolling across the goal mouth and over the line. Keeping up the pressure, Harrison just headed outside from a well placed free kick. A hugs kick by Raynor disposed of an ugly rush on the part of the visitors' forwards, whilst Butler was the means of opening out another hot onslaught on the Rovers' goal. Newton were awarded a penalty for handling by Williams just on the goal line, from which Butler gave Newton the lead. Thus encouraged the hornesters returned to the attack and a splendid effort from Marsh struck the crosabar and rebounded into play to be placed outside by Hill. A good clearance by Le placed the Rovers' forwards in possession and Rankin swung the leather across in perfect fashion, Davies meeting the ball and placing it pat Horrocks, making the scores level after play had been in progress half an hour. From now to the interval play was mostly contested in rnidfivld and the whistle b'ew with the score standing : One goal each. After the resumption Newton seemed to fall away and the Rovers kept them on the defensive almost the whole of the half, the backs having a particularly lively time. However, they managed to keep their opponents out, the final coming with the half-time score unaltered.

Lancashire League.

Warrington Defeated by Rochdale Town.

The first home match of the season was played at Arpley on Saturday, when Warrington encountered Rachdale Town in a Lancashire League fixture. War- rington placed the following team into the field : Goal, G A.thinall ; full hicks, H Cook, and T Dilworth ; half backs, T Eilis, S Dean, and S Hatton; forwards, L Sharplea, J Hammond, J Powell, J Murray, and L Riley, whilst the Rochdale club were represented by Kennedy. goal ; Nightingale and Holmes, full backs ; Wood, Kirkbraght, and Wallwork, half backs; and Boardman, Holden, Goodliff., Leigh, and Horrocks, forwards. Mr Horrocks, Farnworth, near Bolton. was the referee. Powell commenced operations down hill and against the wind, and play was taken to the Roch- dale guar era. The visiting front rank retaliated, but Cook was on the spot and cleared in fine fashion. Goodliffe, however, returned, and two minutes after the start scored a somewhat easy gal. Emboldened by this success Rochdale came sith a rush, but the home backs clearing. Murray passed on to the left to Riley, the ball, however, being sent dead. Rochdale again raced up, but Dilworth relieved, whilst a moment later Holden kicked dead with a huge kick. The visitors again lac,ked dangerous, hut Cook kicked into 'couch. L,igh bad a shot at goal, but kicked high. After good play by Riley, Dilworth was called upon to relieve, and following the kick down Sharpies centred in splendid fashion, but none of the forwards being, up the chance was lost. Powell carried hostilitifs to the Rochdale end and Riley again failed at a pass from Murray, &spinall was then called upon to save, kicking out in clever fashion. Riley, gaining possession, raced down on the left, and centred finely, but the chance was spoiled by Hammond. Shortlyeafterwards Nightingale fouled Riley, but no aavanta,e accrued from the kick. The Rochdale forwards got going, and Dilworth in try- ing to clear kicked over the cross-bar, a corner being awarded, the kick, however, proving futile. Murray gained possessioo, and centred to Powell, but Holmes intercepted, and with a well-judged kick changed the venue. The ball was followed up well by the Rochdale forwards, and from a pass from Leigh in the goal mouth Boardman scored goal number two. Following this Rochdale pleased, but Murray and Riley transferred play. Sharpies ran down on the right, and when near the line sustained a severe itjury to his knee, but pluckily resumed. Aspinall was later on ccmpelled to throw away, and Sharpies, taking up the running, fredueed the visitors' lead with a fine shot. Exciting play followed and shortly after halftime was called with the score:—Rochdale, two goals ; Warring- ton, one goal. On resuming combined play between Wallwork and Kirbbright ended in the latter scoring for the visitors. Play was of a fast and exiting nature, both sets of forwards gaining poem sion. In the later stages Riley played in the centre position, but all to no good, and from a foul in the goal mouth Leigh scored the third point for Rochdale. As time approached, Warrington tried strenuously to place matters mole equal, but all to no purpose, and Rochdale were declared winners by three goals to one goal.

THE CHIEF NUISANCE INSPECTORSHIP.

At a meeting of the Health Committee of the Warrinßton Corporatibn on the recommendation of the Medical Of&er of Health, it was decided to Appoint Inspector Flood, who is fully certi- ficated to act as Chief Inspector of Nuis- ances for a probationary period of three months. Inspector Flood was initiated into the work of this important department of Corpora- tion work by the late Chief Inspector Norman and whilst with him he obtained his certificate as a sanitary inspector. Inspector Flood has shown great aptitude for the work and there is every reason to believe that his probationary trial of three months as chief inspector will Rive satis- faction to the committee. THAT WONDERFUL LEG.

Some time ago a story was narrated in that mod veracidus paper "Truth" about a young man who has the misfortune to be the possessor of a short leg. Num- berless doctors were consulted but all failed to add one cubit to the length of that leg. A lady Chri,- tian (?) scientist was, however, eventually called in. The faith cure was brought into operation and the limb commenced to grow. The lady in the meantime had journeyed abroad to an unknown address. The limb went on growing. The lady could not te found • Therefore the treatment could not be stopped. And the yonzg man is conse- quently in a far worse position to-day than he was twelve months ago. Come ! gather round and list to me, While I tell you of a youth A story which you'll all *wee Is told, at least, " in Truth." Its authenticity is vouched By persons—not a score, Were it in different language couched There would be many more. I want the world to know I tell This melancholy ditty ; Tell all, and when you've tolled the bell You will have told the city. This youth resided far away, By famous London town, And spent the hours from break of day By walking up and down. Sad to relate but still I may Compassion for him win He. at the close of one sad day Was found fast in a gin. Fast held was he—a burning shame— His legs the gin had caught. And when he to his senses came He found one leg was short. The doctors who were called looked grim, -And gazed at him in pain. " You'll ne'er, "still gazing at the limb, " Walk straight on it again." • It was too true his walks he went And got a little stronger, But up and down his body bent, One leg was whole no longer. So in despair he limped to one Far-famed for Christian healing ; She saw his legs once, then was gone, Sweet shyness thus revealing. But with acclaim 'twoulcl be her aim, To make a perfect limb For atsent treatment was the same As present ;she'd cure him. And thus it proved ; alack the day The leg began to grow ; The healer's charm it did obey In thigh, and knee, and toe. It kept on growing,—awm fate,— And caused a huge surprise When found the growth did not abate Nor stop at the right size. His bitter thoughts gave him no peace, And, as a sharp reminder, He advertised, " Toe treatment c-ase," And searched but NUM to find her, Thus failure stared hint in the face, The leg, as though to show It was a most successful case, Continued still to grow. 'Tis at the present7time just on —At least by his own showing Three inc/1s more than Cother one, And still it keeps on growing. The moral to this comedy Remember if you please— It 7 happens oft' the remedy Is worse than the disease. Runcorn A. DODD. -

THdEE SCORE AND TEN.

At the age of seventy-three, Mr Barthn;omew Potts,of Six Houses, Low Westwood, near Enchester,Durbam, is able to work full time after a long illness. His body began to swell, and st times he was in a state of semi-coosciousness. Doctoes medicine did not heln hini ; indeed he grew worse, He started taking Dodd's Kidney Pills, with little or no faith in them. THE VACANCY IN THE APPLE- TON DIVISION.

Election of Mr Roger C Parr.

Five o'clock on Friday was the latest hour at which nominations could be received in connection with the election of a councillor to represent the Appleton Division on the Cheshire County Council, rendered vacant by the elevation ot Mr J C Parr to an aldermanic seat. At the time appointed Mr Henry Greenall, the deputy returning officer at- tended at St. Thomas's Schools, Stockton Heath, for the purpose of receiving nominations Amongst those present were Mr Roger C Parr Mr Lister Kaye, Rev H V Pigot, Mr Vera B Davies, Mr H J Westbrook (election agent for Mr Parr), Mr Rose and Mr Taylor. Four nominations were handed in on behalf of Mr Roger C Parr. In the first paper Mr Parr was proposed by Thomas Joloa Down, seconded by Harry Vernon Pigot, And assented to by James Atherton Gibson, James Whitley, C Hallows, It Shackleton, Henry Isherwood, Ai T Brereton, Samuel I` Welsby, John Fairhurst. In the second paper he was proposed by James llepherd, seconded by John Sutton, Edwin Woods, Frederick Timperley, Frank Lake Good, Frank Warburton, Edwin Brad- shaw, Joseph A McGregor, J Jackson Lee, In the third paper Mr Parr was proposed by Henry secouded by William Pierpoint, supported by W H Woodcock, John Rogerson, Joseph hough. ton Clarke, Thomas Walton, Stanley Wilson, S W Hallow, H Turton, James Ryan. The fourth paper was signed exclusively by ladies. Mr Parr was proposed by Sarah Hewitt, secitaded by Mary Jane Edgerley and supported by Sarah Ann Taylor, Jane Fairhurst, Francis Lanca- shire, Harriet Booth, Mary Burliston, Ann Welbsy, Lucy Wilson, and Eva Twiss. No other candidate being nominated, Mr Parr was declared elected. He was warmly congratula- ted on his return, and in acknowledging the com- pliment, Mr Roger C Parr, C.C„ personally .thanked those present for their assistance, and also all who had contributed to his return.

SEQUEL T• 0 THE WARR!NGTON V BAI LEY FOOTBALL MATCH. " Wattie" Davies Summoned.

At the Warrington County Borough Police Conn on Friday, before the Mayor and other magistrates, Wharton Davies, the well-known Batley three- quarter back,was summoned for assaulting William Brown, 49, Hopwood-street, Warrington,a spectator at the Warrington v Batley football match on the 7th inst.—There was a cross summons against Brown for assaulting Davies,—Mr W J Forshaw appeared for complainant, and Davies was repre- sented by Mr Pearson, of Batley. Mr Forshaw informed the court that Davies had expressed his regret to the complainant and to the Warrington Football Club for his conduct on the ground of the club on the 7th inst, and had agreed to pay all costs and expenses. Under those circum- stances he asked the Bench to allow him to with- draw the summon. Mr Pearson applied for permissicn to withdraw the cross 8UMO:101W The magistrates agreed to the withdrawals.

PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS:

"I SPENT many a sleepless night over that nes, book of mine." "It didn't affect me that way." SHE: "This paper tells of a man who stepped off a cliff and dropped 3049 ft." He "That fellow must be a regular human centipede." HE (very much gone) : "There's nothing half so sweet in life as love's young dream." She: "I don't know. Mixed chocolates are pretty good." MR. RICHFELLO : "Miss De Slimm is evidently a woman of many fine points." Rival Belle: "I should say- so. Did you see her elbows?" "Ronnie," asked the visitor, "have you any brothers and sisters?" "No," replied Robbie, "I'm all the children we've got." • MASTER: "What is an opossum?" Scholar: "A small animal with a pouch." Master: "What does he do with it?" Scholar "When he is hunted he gets inside." MRS. GADABOUT; "Our new servant is a ewel. -She cooks beautifully, is very economical, never roes out and never talks back." Ittteto to the abater.

Election of Mayor. Sir,—ln the petition which has been promoted re- questing Mr Parr to become Mayor of Warrington, or rather to allow his name to be put forward for nomira- tion,l notice the name of a firm in which I am interested viz. : The Union Tannf ries Limited, and as Managing Director ef that Firm, I hasten to repudtv.te its inclu- sion on the list named. There is no need for me to say anything about the methods used to obtain signatorps, but this one instance proves that names want weighihy as well as countidy. Yours truly. ROBERT H. POSNETT, Managing Director Union Tanneries Ltd.

Sir,—l do not wish to enter into the controversy as to the selection of Mayor from a party point of view. The Conservatives have a majority in the Council and the greater part of them have thought fit to exercise their right of selection in inviting Mr Parr. It would be unreasonable to object to the action of the Conser- vative Party or to the adoption by thorn of all fair means of inducing Mr Parr to accept the office. Ido however take exception to the mis-representations con- cerning Mr Roberts which have ben made in inducing myself and others to sign the petition to Mr Parr. I am convined that there is some motive under-lyrng the action of those opnosed to Mr Roberts and I should likeito ask why it was thought necessary to advertise the petition to Mr Parr in one of your local contemporaries on Saturday last and who paid the cost of :he ad- vertisement 2—Yours truly, FAIR PLAY.

Sir,—l do not sopipcse that either the Mayor or Mr Charlton Parr will care to have th.iir qualifications and claims debated in a newspaper correspondence. It wou'd seem right, however, that Mr Charles Finch who wrote in your last week's issue should be told that he is under a misapprehension in thinking that Mr Parr is unacquainted with the work of local government. Be ought to be aware that Mr Parr has been the representative on the Cheshire County Council of the Appleton Electoral Division (embracing 15 parishes) ever since County Coun-ils were con- stituted by the Local Government Act, 1888. Moreover that such have been Mr Fair's services on that Council that some years ago he was asked, but declined in favour of a colleague, to accept a County Aldermanship. A few months ago however on a re- newal, and on his acceptance of the request Mr Parr was unanimously elected to that important office. Surely Mr Fitch must admit that membership of the Cheshire County Council (one of the most important in England) for so long a period, and especially the active service which Mr Parr has devoted to County Local Government, and the fact that his colleagues have unanimously elected him a County Alderman which involves the presidency over deliberations and details quite as important as those of our town, form some guarantee that he is qualified to preside over the local government of a County Borough. At all events the vast body of our townsfolk think so. In any case Mr Parr is in thia respect certainly as fitted as was the Duke of Norfolk who on a special occasion was made Mayor of Sheffield, and the Marquis of Bute who on a special occasion was made Mayor of Cardiff—probably considerably more so.—Yours &c., J. MARSH, Sankey-itreet, Warrington.

The Cobra Disaster.

Sir,—l feel it my duty, through the medium of your i columns, to call attention to the terrible stress which must necessarily reoult from the lrg4l of H.M.S. 1 "Cobra.' At least 67 officers and men were drowned, of whom 44 belonged to the Royal 11-.vy. I would remind you that the scale of pensions which the Admiralty provide is altogether inadequate. They range from 6. 6d. to 39. 61 p:_ir week for widows, with two shillings or eighteennevce per week for each chili. Obviously it is simply imp( ssible for a poor woman to maintain herself and her family respectably on such a sum. The Admiralty Pensions must therefore be augmented from outside sources, and it is to procure the money to enable this to be done that I am making this appeal to the inhabitants r f this great Empire. As the less of B.M.S. " Cobra " 13 a National disaster I feel sure that the Nation will recognise its duty towards those who have not only lost their loved ones, but who will now have to face a grinding poverty unless the country in whose service their husbands or fathers lost their lives comes forward to their assistance. I shall be pleased to acknowledge any donations for- warded to me ac the Town Ball, Portsmouth, and can assure you that the amount collected will be so adminis- tered as to afford the greatest relief where it is most needed.—Yours faithfully, A. LEON EMANUEL. Mayor. The Town Ball, Portsmouth, 25th Sept., 1901.

• 3.: THE NEW ELECTRIC EXPRESS.

Plans of the Promoters.

The progress of the new railway from Manellester to Liverpool. powers for the construction of which were obtained in the last session of Parliament, has,a " Mac- cheater Evening News" representative ascertained, been absolutely nil up to the present, but the plans of the promoters for the immediate future cannot fail to have much interest for the inhabitants of the two towns chir fly concerned, as well for those of the district on each side of Warrington through which the railway is to pass. The latter will, in all probability, see trains running on the new mono-rail line much sooner than will residents in either Manchester or Liverpool. Of course the first thing to be accomplished is to raise the capital. This will be done by the issue of a prrspectus offering to the public shares in , a new company to be promoted by the syndicate who ' obtained the Act recently passed. A meeting of the promoters will be called sometime towards the end of next month for the purpose of considering this and a number of other matters of pressing importance. As to the raising of the capital required there is no doubt. Indeeci.two or three offers have already been made both to Mr Behr and to :others of the promoters of the new line for the whole Of the capital required, but the latter have me Intention of letting the new mcnc-railway be built by other than Lancashire enterprise, and they are full of confidence that Lancashire will supply all the money needed without any real application to Lsndon or the country generally'

===== 0002601_19010927_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ATTITUDE CF SALFORD.

There are other matters needing a speedy settlement, and one of these is the Bill which is to be promoted during the enluing session of Parliament. This is recessitatrd by the compromise arrived at with Salford when the Bill was before Sir Lewis M'lver's Committee. Curiously enough the result of the compromise will be to save the company about £lOO,OOO, which will pay the cost of the Expensive opposition in Parliament about twice over. One element of the compromise is the shortening of the line by a slight deviation in Salford. This shorter route through the borough was originally contemplated by Mr Behr in making his survey and preparing the plans for the new line, but there were so many objections that could have been urged against it from a public point of view that tte idea was aban- doned in favour of the scheme as it came before Parlia- ment. When the Corporation of Salford commenced to discuss details it was found that rather than accept the route laid down they were willing that Tatton-street should be closed for a couple of years while excavations and bridges were being made in preference to the company taking possession of the large blocks of property which would be rr quired if the work projected under the 1901 Act was constructed. Such a Bill will not be oppoePd by Salford, and probably not by any body, wkile the line will be straightened as it apprcaches the Manchester terminal station. The solicitors and the engineer are now engaged in preparing the Bill and plans for this deviation.

WHEN THE NEW LINE_ WILL PROBABLY_ RE BEADY.

The public will probably be pleased to learn that it.is not expected that much more than three years will elapse before the line will be opened, and it is hoped that work on it will he commenced early next spring. Mr Behr has all along urged on the promoters the desir- ability of making a start at three pointslalong the line simultaneously—at Warrington, Liverpool, and Man- chester, The real difficulty both of construction and in the acquisition of the necessary property will he at the two termini. The land in the open country being easy to acquire and easy to wcik on will be first utilised, and it is expected that the generating station at Warrinaton and the line itself between Garston at one end and the Bridgewater Canal at the other will be pushed forward very rapidly and probably completed by the spring of 1903. By the time this is done it islex- pected that a coupleof cars will be completed according to the designs which were presenttd to the committees of the Houses of Parliament last session, and in these experiments will be made on the 24 miles of line so corn- pleted by which it will be Optermined whether any, and if so,what improvements will be needed in the construc- tion of the remainder of the rolling stock. Twelve months of experiment will solve many knotty problems and will teach much, and after this period has elapsed there will still be ample time to order new rolling stock in accordance with the designs that will be evolved as the result of this lengthy period of experiments, The Board of Trade officials will be able at this time to suggest any improvements which may occur to them to mitigate or avoid any of the dangers that may then have been demonstrated. Thus by the time the more difficult portions of the line in Manchester ana Liverpool:have been constructed every detail will have been settled, so that there may be no de/ay in opening the new line almost immediately after it is completed. THE RECEPTION OF TENDERS. In reference to the announcement which appeared in several papers recently to the effect that tenders for the construction of the new line would shortly be opened it is authoritatively stated that the question of asking for or receiving tenders has neveri yet received the i attention of the directors, and t s not at all probable that it will do so unti; after the new capital is raised.

THE EXAMINER FRIDAY, SEPTEMBER 27, 1901 OUR LOCAL CHURCHES. EARLESTOWN & NEWTON •1

ST. JOHN'S CHURCH, EARLESTOWN.

Special services were held in this church on Sun- day in aid of the day school funds. The Rev J C Matthews preached at the morning service, and the Vicar, Rev T R Matthews, in the evening. There were large congregations, and substantial collec- tions were made for the schools. VIADUCT INSTITUTE BOWLING CLUB.

A handicap for Mr A Tunstall's annual prize should have been pa 3 ed on Saturday, but in con4e- quence of the rain, it had to be postponed. The knife and fork tea, which was arranged for, as a wind-up to the handicap, was, however, proceeded with, and thoroughly enjoyed by thoee who were present. A convivial evening was afterwards spent, songs being given by several of those present. Sr. PETER'S CHORAL SOCIETY NEWTON.

The annual meeting of this Socisty was held on Monday evening in St. Peter's school. Mr E Ley- land was appointed conductor; Rev James Ryder. chairman: H F Houghton, treasurer ; and G Collinge, secretary. Misses Adams and Baybut, far Newton, Mr R Carson, for Wargrave, were appoin- ted as a committee, the appointment of an Earles- town representative being left over. The fee has been increased from 23 to 3s, the members to pro- vide their own music. NEWTON-LE-WILLAYS BOWLING CLUB, The annual handicap for Mr A C McC3rquodale's prize and the entrance fees was commenced on Saturday. There were 64 entries. The handicap is divided into two sections, and all the players have to play on what is called the flat green, an I afterwards on the crown green. Os Saturday play was on the flat green, and resulted in J Bennett taking premier honours, which ensures him a second prize, although he still has another chance for the first prize on the crown green. L Bartlett was runner-up. WAIIGRAVE CTITIRCFI.

The alterations in connection with this church are now well advanced, and it is expected that it will be ready for the resumption of the ordinary services in about three weeks. The whole of the old windows have been replaced by new ones of a very neat design, all the old plaster has been removed from the walls, which ha ce been re- plastered, whilst for abut three feet high round the wane woolen panelling nicely varnished has replaced theplaster. The seating has been altered and instead of the straight backed uncomfortable pews, a lean back is provided, and all the divisions taken out. The organ has been taken down and will be thoroughly cleaned and renovated, and a new stop added. The total cost will be something approaching .£l,OOO, but the adde I comfort and cleanly appearance of tue church will fully com- pensate for the cost.

A RUNAWAY HORSE AT EARLESTOWN -AN OLD MAN INJURED. _

On Tuesday afternoon consPerable excitement was clused in Bridge-street, Earlestown, thr migh a horse belonging to the Co-operative Society, becom- ing frightened and running away. The horse was attached to an empty cart, and when crossing the bridge which spans Messrs Evans and Co's. private line, it suddenly bolted and raced at a goad speed down Bridge-street. On reaching Queen-street it attempted to turn,but swerved on to the footpath, and one of the wheels came into violent contact with the door of Mr E Goslin's shop. The wheel was forced off, and the cart fell over, this bringing the horse ton stand. An old man named Maynes, living in Derby-street, and aged 71 years, was standing near the shop, and unable to escape the danger, he was knocked down, lie was seriously injured and was conveyed on the ambulance to Dr. Valentine's where his injuries were attended to,and he was afterwards taken home in a cab. The damage to Mr Goslin's shop is considerable, one large window being demolished,and a good quantity of provisions destroyed. EARLESTOWN FOOTBALL CLUB.

A concert in aid of the funds of this club was givea in the Town Hall, Earlestovvn, on Monday evening. The room was well tilled and the funds of the club will no doubt benefit considerably. With one or two excsptions, local artistes were engaged, but they all acquitted themselves admirably, and to the delight of the audience. The progromme was Agreeably diversified, the glees of the Mazawattee Glee party being heartily appre- ciated. Their items were " 0 who will o'er the downs," " Patenc medicine," " Boys of the .old brigade," and " Wake little Kate," Miss Lottie Jones sang very nicely the two items put down to her account and Mr J Isherwood gave much pleas- ure by his manipulation of the musical glasses. He also proved himself a capable humorist, his contri- butions "In the twilight," and Girls I have met" being well received. Mr Fred Marigold reeeived full appreciation for his songs " Afton Water," and " The sailor's grave." Mr Walter Limond was heartily applauded for the song "soldier Jim," which was given in masterly style. Miss Lottie Jones and Mr W Litnond gave a nice rendering of the duet, " When the wind blows in from the sea," and Mr M alsh'e manipulation of the cornet in a couple of eolos " Old folks at home,' and " Annie Laurie," was loudly applauded. Mr W Teesdale presided at the piano, and also contributed a solo.

NOTES BY THE wAY AT EARLESTOWN.

[By A CORRESPONDENT]. FOOTBALL.

As a result of Sr R.dens Town and Wigan United having t ) replay their Lancashire Senior Cup Tie, Earlestown were left without a match on Saturday. Tois will enable them to get their best available team together for their encounter with Darwen to- morrow, for there is no aoubt but that they win need it. Darwen are always a dangerous team on their own ground, and they are showing such in- vincible form this season that it is extremely unlikely that Earlestown will make much impres- sion on them .A team that can beat Blackburn Rovers league team in a Cup tie—and this is what Darwen diri a fortnight ago—is indeed a team to be feared However, Earlestown have got their best available team together, and they may spring a pleasant sur- prise on us by walking off with one if not both of the points. Stranger things than this have hap- pened in football. A new man will be seen et ceatre-torward in the Earlestown in the person of Dawson, late of Everton, whilst a new outside left is expected to turn out. Dawson is an accomplish- ed player, and EarleQto wn have done a good stroke of business in obtaining his signature, With these two new forwards and Bartley back in his old position the Earlestown forward line should prove a formidable one.

Newton were again at home on Saturday, the visitors being Tranmere Rovers. The game during the first halt was exciting enough,but after the inter- val both teams fell away Oonsiderably,and the play became just as dull as it had previously been ex- hilarating. The final score was one goal each and this about represented the game although the Rovers several times looked like booking the full comple- ment of points, as they did almost the whole of the pressing during the second nalf, only a determined defence keeping them at bay; The Newton for- wards did not exhibit near as much dash as they did against Birkenhead, in fact the might not have been the same set. They played in disjointed fashien, and seemed utterly at sea when they got in front of goal. Hill at centre-forward was a miserable failure, and this seemed to affect the play of the rest of the men. Rattling centres which any youngster with a knowledge of the game could have turned to account, went begging times with- out number. Newton will have to make drastic alterations in their forward line if matches are to be won.

The Rovers met. with another disaster on Satur- day, when Birchley defeated them by three goals to one. It is pretty evident that the Rovers are not up to last vear's standard as yet, two points out of a possible six will never do,

NEW YORK'S POPOLATION.—The city of NOW York contains twice as many Irish as any cite in Ireland,and a greater number of Germans than any city in Germany with the sole exception of Berlin. Many thnussnds of its inhabit-ants cannot speak English. Only 20 per cent of the New Yorkers are American by birth and Parentage, and of them only one in eight is of New Yoik descent.

The Rev. J. F. Matthews on Street

Betting and Sunday Trading.

Preaching at Latchford Baptist Church on Sunday morning from the words" Righteousness exalteth a nation, but sin is a reproach to any people "—Prov. xiv, 34.. the Rev. J. F. Matthews said : No citizen of this ancient borough ought to feel any other than .gratetul to the Chief Constable for the bold and energetic way in which he has recently dealt with the growing nuisance of street gambling. I am pleased to see that be contemplates still more vigor- ous measures if his action has not the desired effect upon the fraternity. More than that, I think that he should kaow that he has the suppert of all right- feeling and loyal Churchmen in his effort to keen the streets of Warrington as morally sweet as possible. The unblushing effrontery of these who tout for bets is simply notorious. One can see these men on the Kautsford-road and in D diem- lane, and other places any day of the week, while they keep a vigilant eye upon any police officer that may happen to be in the neighbourhood. Youths and lads, girls and young women, are easily drawn to offer a bet, and no one can tell the final result of the evil spirit which gambling engenders. There is another matter of intere-st which has been talked about for some time oast, I arn glad to say. Teat is the opening of shops. There are not many on this side Black Bear Bridge, but on the other side, and in Warrington altogether the number seems very large. No value whatever is set upon the day, and no attempt is made to observe the usual decorum associated therewith. The opening of shops on the part of petty shopkeepers is ethically wrong and economically uesound. Ethically wrong because actually unfair to ocher tradesmen, economically un- sound because for purposee of labeer it meaus a seven days' working week. The spirit which prompts all this is morally bad. Greedeust of gain is at the bottom of it all, the unhealthy appetite to get money at all cost. Fl)vr far-reaching the powers of our local authorities extend I do not yet fully know, but here again I think if they would use what they already po,sess they would have the active supp:rt of all the churches, and a large, a very large, proportion of the ratepayers. That they do enjoy some power is obvious from the attempt m ide to put down street criers on Sunday, and to limit the sale of milk to certain hours in the forenoon of the same day. But for the most part the creation of an ideal moral atmosphere are the best, the true6t and the surest means by which any reform may be brought about. Savonarola's attempt to improve the morals of the Florentines failed because it left the hearts of the people just where they were. Had they been alive to the redeeming pawere of Christ's love, had the Florentine conscience been made eensitive to the enormity and heinousness of sin as God views it, Sayonarola'd reform would have been carried through without any dangerous reaction. We need to raise the standard of righteousnees high among the people of our town, not to lower it. To do that would be to bring (ur whole teaching into ridicule and contempt. If the ideal is to live and grow so must there be an atmosphere at Once healthy arid congenial. The public mind must be persuaded both of the vitality and reality of our religious life. They must see that it is both strong and free and sweet. Then the necessary influence will he felt. There will be a slow but positive deepening of conviction that " Rignteoueness exalteth a nation, but sin is a reproach to any people."

The Ex-president of the Conference at Bold-street Wesleyan Church.

The visit of the Rev Thomas Allen, D.D., the ex- President of the Wesleyan Conference, to B'U-street Church on Sunday had the effect of attracting large congregations. The services are known as the "Chapel Anniversary services, and the collections taken were to meet the diluent expenses of the church. The con- gregation at the morning service was an excellent one, and the singing was of a healty character. The rev. gentleman spoke from the words "Time that dwellest in the gardens, the companions hearken to Thy voice : cause me to hear it," from which he preached a practical discourse. The choir rendered the anthem "Send out Thy light" (Gounod) in a praiseworthy manner, the organ being ably presided over by Mr F Hickman. At the evening service the building was crowded. Dr Allen spoke from several sentences in the 144th Psalm and applied them to a large number of everyday subjects. Speaking especially to the young men from the words " That our sons may he as plants grown up in their youth," he said that he saw a large number of young men in the congregation. Might be ask them if the King's figure of speech illustrated their personal experi- ence ? Toe strength of the nation was in its family life and woman was the corner stone of the home, The de- velopments of commerce in England during the last century has saved them as a nation from absolute starvation, and their gratitude was due to Almighty God- That marvellous commercial supremacy would not be maintainea without a great struggle. It was a long time since any foreign ft tg had waved over the heads of Eaglishmen, and if they combined wisdom with righteousness in their foreign policy they would be able to disarm the miserable poiey of other foreign powers. —The choir, which was an augmented one of over 60 voices, performed a very difficult task with praiseworthy smartness. The special music set out for the evening was from the " Messiah " : Surely he bath borne." " And with His stripes," and " All we like sheep," and they were presented in a masterly manner. Great praise is due to Mr Hickman, whose efficient accom- paniment conduced so much to the success of the day. A collection was taken on behalf of he trust funds and the service closed with the pronouncing of the Bene- diction.

KINK ET-STREET. Warrin;gton.—(d.) - Union of Lancashire and Cheshire Institutes The .ordinary monthly meeting of the Council was held at the Memorial Hall, Manchester, on Tuesday, Mr W T Postleth waits presiding. On the motion of the Chairman, the following address was ordered to be sent to the United States Ambassa- dor :—" The C3uncil of the Union of Lancashire and Cheshire Educational Institutes, which Union has a membership of over 163,000, with upwards of 130,700 students under instruction at evening classes, desires to record its deep sympathy with Mrs McKinley and the citizens of the United States of America in the great sorrow which she and they have sustained .by the untimely death of President McKinley. The late President, representing as he did a large section of the Anzlo-Saxon race with whom Great Britian is associated by relationship and by a common literature. filled the exalted office of Chief Magistrates of the United States of ' America with worthy dignity; this Council, as representative of education in this populous centre of the United Kingdom, deplores the act which brought about his death, and trusts the day may soon dawn when true education and thought shall so permeate the mind of man that such outrages may be no more." Mr Fred Pickford of the Royal Technical institute, Salford, was nominated for the acceptance of the Owens College Senate as " Rnm- ney " scholar; the" Mather" exhibition of £lO per annum for three years, was awarded to Mr Leon- ard Bingham of the Municipal School of Techno- logy, Manchester.
