IWKINLEY'S MURDERER.

Tried and Found Guilty.

The trial of Czolgoiz for the murder of President M.Kinley wss concluded on Tuesday at Buffalo. ...Police and other officials who were on duty in the Temple of Music when the crime was committed described what they saw, and one witness, a member of the Reception Committee, proved the prisoner's confession while in the hands of the police. He said Czelgoiz answered questions freely, and declared that he considered he had done his duty. Ho did not seem to be mentally dis- turbed. Dr Mann in cross .eicarnination said the Presi• dent was somewhat weakened by want of air and things of that kind, and the fact undoubtedly had something to do with the result, but there was nothing known to medical men that would have saved him,

:several of the guards and civilians who were near : the President also gave evidence in connection with the shooting of Mr M`Kinley and occurrences following. One of these was a negro named Branch. who was employed at the exhibition in thq Temple of Muiic. Branch, replying to an inquiry frr m Mr Titus whet her he heard the President say anything after he was shot, said, " Yes. I saw and heard him say, Be easy with him, boys.'" Ccunsel for the defence complained of the sudden closing of the trial, and said the prismer had stead- fast!: efused to give them any asistanse. There were no witnesses to be called for the defence. It was stag. gusted to the jury that it would be a great relief if they could find that the accused was a madman and not an assassin' The jury, after deliberating for over half an hour, found a verdict of murder in the first degree— that is, with premeditation and intent to kill.

NOTES ON UAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL,

By " Ovalist."

Language almost fails me in attempting to describe the encounter between Warrington and Broughton Rangers last Saturday. The match VIM magnificent, the result was agonising. Certainly Warrington never had harder luck in my recollection of them. They man- fully withstood the onslaughts of the Ringers when the latter were fresh and bad the wind behind them ; they gradually got the upper hand, they played the home team almost to a standstill, and then they lost the game by a single point in the last minute of the game. It was cruel luck. You cculd no more blame Warrington than you could give the home team credit. Personally I never felt more savage with fate and the accursed eccentrici- ties of football:than I did on Saturday evening—no, not even after the cup-final last season. Die Rangers have loomed so large in the estimation of the prophets that for Warrington to have beaten them on their own turf would have been a startling triumph. And as far as play is concerned Warrington 'were the winners ! Widdeson's last effort was splendid, but it does not rob Warrington of the honour of having quite over-played and morally beaten the Rangers at Broughto“. Few clubs—if any—will visit Wheater's Field this season and do so well as did the Warrington lads on Saturday, I always providing, of course, that the Rangers keep up their present form.

Warrington went emboldened by previous Successes and determined to win. As regards determination the same may be said of the Rangers, who had so far won all their engagements in most decisive fashion. Tioey started off too in a style which encouraged hopes ot yet another victory in the minds of their spectators. The visitors were very soon penned to their line, and the home pasiing machinery was in full swing. Notwith- standing the wet bail, the two James's, Wilson and Graham handled the ball very accurately, and it was a marvel how the last-named pair were kept out. They gave the Warrington left wing a lot to do, and as usual that wing was perfectly reliable. Fish tackled in wonder- ful fashion and more than once he had two men to face. They never passed him however and it was due to his excellent work that the oefence was not pierced more than once in the first half. o:casionally the Warrington halves and centres were called upon to tackle and right well did they do it, but generally when the Rangers got the ball they passed it along to the Wilson-Graham wing. In fact the monotony of tha play in this direc- tion was mistaken policy for the Rangers, Warrington being always ready beforehand for the attacking move- ment. Widdeson on the other side had little to do, but he did it well.

It was not all defence fr Warrington and they made a-number of smart movements which carried play into the Rangers' territory. Long punting with the wind always drove them back, however, and the home team bad certainly the lion's share of the game up to the in— terval. Just before changing ends .Fish sustained an injury to his lez,which resulted in him being assisted from tlae field of play to the great disappointment of ths five hundred Warringtonians who' were present. That disappointment soon turned to ill, when he re- sumed after halt time.

The wind slight as it was,!made considerable differ- ence to the play and Warrington were soon attacking as hotly as the Rangers had been earlier on. Five minutes of the second moiety had not elapsed before Allen cleverly dribbled the ball through the home backs and Fish completed the effort by sprinting over the line !arid scoring the first point of the match. Lame he certainly was,but for a lame man he travelled remarkably quickly when he saw the bail cross the goal line. This reverse naturally roused the Rangers up and they immediately made tracks for the Warrington line. W James initiated a smart series of exchanges which ended in Hogg easily making the scores equal. Then it was a ding-dung struggle. Warrington played like heroes and completely wore down their opponents. A fierce attack was main- tained. Fish failing to score only by inches after a dash- ing run to the corner. Then Allen crossed the line only to be brought back and Hallam had an unsuccessful drop at goal. Soon after he put his aide ahead with a beautifully judged long low penalty shot which just cleared the pa! the home team got very groggy " as Warrington improved, and were all over beaten in the last quarter, Both the passing and punt- ing were had, while Warrington were superb all round. Hogg made a mis-kick which accessed the ball across the field, i.nd from this blunder the Rangers won the game. Widdeson got hold when he had nobody facing him, and before anybody could realise it he was speeding I for the line with everybody in pursuit. Be rounded I Harris and Hallam, and scored a sensational winning try right at the corner, amid the frantic yells of the Broughton spectators. A minute before they were in the dumps and cursing every man on the home side. Now they were delirious with joy and thought there was , no tsam on earth like the Rangers, while little Widde- son only needed a pair of wings and he would soar straight to Heaven.

For all that they admitted that Broughton never had a luckier win, and so say I. It was luck pure and simple, though the sc)rer deserves every ounce of praise he gets. Warrington were very much cut up about it, the players saying they never played a harder game in their lives. It was certainly a tremendous struggle between two well.balanced teams from first to last. Play was always of a high order and up to the best standard of the Northern Union.

--- Though the afternoon was wet and drizzly the ground was in fine order. Somebody seems to have at last solved the secret of growing grass at Lower Broughton, for there is plenty on the field now. There was a splen- did "gate ' too, about 9,000 people surrounding the enclosure in order to witness tw) undefeated teams endeavour to slay one another.

All dab home teams won on Saturday, but one at least did not deserve to do so.

Comparing the Warrington and Broughton teams is no easy task. Where one was superior the other was inferior. Warrington had a big pull at full back where Hallam gave a masterly display, and on form I doubt if he has an equal in Lancashire. The forwards were also slightly bett,r than the opposing eight, but there was not a great deal in it. The Rangers were best represented at half-back,'whers W James did some very fine work. He is just the class of man Warrington could do with. Allen and Burgess are " good plucked 'uns," but neither is fast enough. The first named was a long way the best of the two last Saturday, the Fliaton youth being poor on the attack. The two three quarter lines gave (xcellent displays, and though the Rangers quartette attacked more scientifically it was due to the work of the halves. Nothing could have been finer than the defence of the Warrington backs in the first half and tney all combined effcctively at times. With a class half•back to ep3n the game out they need fear nothing in the country. Where is he?

This Sraturday the amnia( battle with Runcorn takes place at Wilderspool and it no secret that Warrington Mean to win. They have put up with defeats from the " Linnets " long enough and the tide is timed to turn somewhere between 330 and 5 o'clock this Saturday afternoon. Runcorn have been . very lucky in all their recent engagements with Warrington and I hope the luck will turn with the tide. In the way of ill-luck Warrington had enough last Saturday to laat this season through. Given a fine day the game should prove a big draw in this part of Lancashire and Cheala're. Both sides play open football and each has only once been defeated in this season's competition.

The Warrington team to meet Runcorn was chosen on Wednesday evening and is the strongest .t he Club can possibly put in the fold. Scholtze has been given a rest, Boardman making a welcome re-appearance. The fifteen is composed as follows :—Hallam, full back ; Fish, Isherwood, Dickenson and Harris, tt,ree quart.!rs ; Allen and Burgess. half backs ; B )ardman, Felt, Eden. Edmondson, Morrison, Cunningham, J Swift and F Swift, forwards.

To show the difficulty there is in getting rllyers of any note I may just mention that one of the Cumberland clubs asked Warrington £lOO for the transfer of a well- known forward. Cool, isn't it ? We should expect half a dczen pughers for that figure.

" Wattle" Davies is not content with having got off so lightly at the Warrington Police Court. On Saturday evening he sent a letter to the "Yorkshire Evening Post" which gave an ingeniously garbled account of the whole matter. Reading it one might imagine that Davies was the injured party, for he talks largely about the summons which he had taken out against a War- rington spectator. Then he blames the Warrington Committee for giving tbe afZair as much publicity as possible, and finally winds up by asserting that the Warrington spectators are the worst in England and warns the Yorkshire clubs to keep a guard over their players when they come to Warrington. Well done " Wattle " Davies! fie would take some beating in the delicate art of distorting the truth.

--- However he has not yet heard the last of the matter. The Warrington club is not going to sit down under imputations of the kind Davies has published abroad.

A ST HELENS SCHOLAR WITH A BROKEN SKULL. Mr Brighouse, county coroner, held an inquest et Peasley Cross, St Helens, on Monday evening, on the', body. of James Leonard Leyland, aged thirteen, wllo. died on Friday, as a result of injuries received wage.; playing in St Anne's Cathelic Schoolyard, Sutton, August 22ad. Deceased's father, Thomas W Leyland.' of the Slat. Inn, Baxter's-lane, said be did not know 0_ the accident until the evening of Tuesday, September Y. when the lad, who slept with him, groaned when in bed. He said he had a lump on his head caused bl pulling at the handle of a door, which broke off and le him down heavily. Dr Felton saw him, and round bee had been going about all this time with a fracture* skull. Dr Bates said the lad died from an abscess 0 the brain, raised by the inflammation at the fracture. . The jury returned a verdict of " Accidental Death." ELECTRIC CABLE SCARE.—PALL OF A TROLLEY POLS' Great alarm was caused in Cnurch-stree:, St. Helena./ a few minutes past nine on Sunday night, IoY a grea electric flare, which proceeded from the tramway cur, rent wire and one of the trolley poles, which for a brief space of time was illuminated with the electric curreat. This pole, situated at the corner of Church-street ana Naylor-street, supported a large electric arc lamp, an. by some means the top of the trolley pole was broke off abut four feet down, and the arc lamp, with th ornamental finial and part of the pole, fell wit, a crash on to the pavement below. The stree^ was crowded with the usual Sunday night crowd mostly of young people, and several passers-b had very narrow escapes from the fall of the beav cast-iron fittings and lamp, which crashed down with, out the slightest warning. The people in the street rad, terror-stricken in all directions. Toe five arc lamps on., the affected circuit were extinguished and the street'', plunged into darkness. It was at first nought that, something had gone wrong with the trolley wire,'but it seemed that some of the guard wires, which are placed' above the live wire to preveot telegraph and telephone-. wires falling on the trolley wire, had come into contact with the live wire, and carried the full force of tee current on to the trolley pole the top of which broke eff and precipitated the arc lamp into the street. The electric power station Was at once communicated with by an employee healed Green, who happened to be the street and witnessed the whole occurreuce. YEOMANRY CHURCH PARADE. Shepherds' Conference. The annual meeting of the Lancashire and adjacent counties of the Loyal Order of Ancient Shepherds, A LT, was held on Saturday evening at Chorley, when thirty delegates were present from Manchester, Warrington, Golborne, St Helens, Chorley and other places. The objects of the coe-. ference are to extend the Order, to raise decayinf lodges, and secure the unification of members' res presentatives at the A3l C. Another object is to,, further the establishment of a convalescent bola!, for sick members. There are 13 districts associatecli, with the movement, representing 7,378 memberssl, In his report, the secretary (Brother Jones, of Man' chester) stated that the work of the past year svB9' most satisfactory, and that there was a good balance in hand. Rules were adopted, and the officials elected were as follows ;—Bro Gartside, president ; Bro W H Sugden (Stalybridge), vice- president ; and Bra C A Jones, secretary. A noir' mittee of five were elected and three trustees,while the next meeting was fixed to be held at Bolton.

ELECTRIC LIGHTING AT WARRINGTON. Proposed . Extensions. Local Government Board Inquiry Yesterday. An inquiry was conducted at the Town Hall, Warrington, on Friday morning by Mr M K North, A.M.I.C.E , Local Government Board Inspector, with regard to the application of the Corporation for powers to borrow £15,000 for electric lighting purposes. There were present :—Alderman Shaw Green (chairman of the Finance and Estate Com- mittee), Messrs J Lyon Whittle (town clerk), S Wilson (deputy town clerk), J Fairhurst (borough treasurer), W H rimsdale (electrical engineer), Major Cardew (consulting engineer), C Madeley (librarian),T Longdinr(borough surveyor), W Boyle (borough treasurer's department), and E Ewan 1 (contractor for the tramways). There was no opposition to the application. The TOWN CLERK, in answer to a question by the Inspector, said the population of the borough at the last census was 64,242. The BOROUGH TREASURER then explained to the In- spector that the assessable value of the Borough was £212,853 and the balance ontatandha,g on loans under tee Sanitary Acts was £208,434. the INSPECTOR What period do you ask for the loan ? The BOROUGH TREASURER: As long a term as the Board will allow. The INSPECTOR : What period was allowed for the electric lighting station ? The Boreouoa TREASURER : Twenty five years. The Towle CLERK pointed out, in explaining the case for the Corporation,that the application was made under the Warrington Electric Lighting Order of 1898. The Local Government Board had already sanctioned the borrowing of £44,966 which had been spent in the erection of a generating station and plant and the supply and laying of malts for the whole of the com- pulsory area of supply. Major CARDEW Also the public lighting of streets. The TOWN CLERK said the application for the addi Monet loan was to enable the Corporation to meet the growing requiremeM.s of the town. Mr WILLIAM HENRY GRIMSDALE, engineer in the elec- tricity department of the Corporation, then explained in detail the extensions it was proposed to carry out at the station and the new maine proposed to be added to those already existing. The following figuees show the increased demand for eleetric light since the sys- tem was inaugurated in December last. At the end of January the demand was equal to 516 'eight-candle power lamps ; February 1.223 : March, 7.186 ; April, 7,416 ; May, 8441 ; June, 8,533: August, 10,652 and September, 10,910. It was sate to assume that the present rate of increase would con- t melee' The Buziopore TREASURER .s:atel that the amount spent up to date was £40,653. There were certain liabilities besides. The INSPECTOR What about the financial aspect of this undertaking ; is it going to pay well ? The BOROUGH TREA,URER : I think so. The INSPECTOR : As Tar as you can judge from your short experience of it ? The BOROUGH TREASURER C think in about two years from now it will pay. Mr Alderman SHAW GREEN: I can assure you we are looking with great interest at the financial aspect of it. The INSPECTOR : What is the charge to customers ? Mr GRIMSDALE : We charge 6i for the first one hour and a half and 3cl per hour afterwards. Mr Alderman Snaw GREEN (TO the engineer): Suppose the present rate of increase nontieues, tor how long will the proposed extensions suffice ? Mr GRIMSDALE: We are hoping 7that the plant will last us this winter and the next. The INSPECTOR: 11) you expect, the increased de- mend to centinue. Mr GRIMSDALE: The increase shows an average of 1,000 !Wan per month. Tnere is every reason to les- lieve tett the increased demand will continue,especially with the extension of the mains. We have also to pre- pare for the trarnways. Major CARDEW said there was no doubt that the true 'way to make the undertaking a financial success was to look forward. If they once let their plant get inade- quate and overtaxed it took a long time to get equal to Itoe demand. Many times he nal known of authorities 'getting into great difficulties through not looking suffie ciently ahead. It was the right policy to have th proper reserve plant to meet all demande. Mr Alnerman SHAW GREEN : I think that policy is one we have adopted. Major CARDEW : Just eo. Continuing, he said he fully endorsed the nolluY of getting this additional plant at Warri, gton. He thought they were getting it at the right time and it would enable them to supply customers for some time to come. If they ran their plant without reserve it wee a terrible time fir the engineer and if there was a break down there was great dissatisfaction They could not expect this new enterpriza to get to a stage of finality at once or even to get to the stage of paving a large profit immediately. Alderman GREEN : When you have ratepayers to face you want to make the best arrangements you can. The inquire was then closed The INSPECTOR afterwards proceeded to the electric ghting station.

THE WATERWORKS EXTENSIONS. Visit of the Corporation. On Friday afternoon the members of the Town Council were afforded an opportunity of inspecting the Corporation Waterworks and the impor- tant extensions now in progress. It will be remembered that at the last meeting of the Council, Councillor William Roberts spoke of th 3 advisability of members of the Council being made acquainted with the various undertakings of the Corporation n6w in pro- gress. He then suggested that the young mem- bers of the Council should be invited to inspect the waterworks and other municipal works. The Water Committee has been the first to take the hint and yesterday afternoon the whole of the members of the Council were invited to visit the works at Appleton and Winwick. Among those who availed themselves of the invitation were the Mayor (Alderman Roberts), Aldermen Sutton and Bolton. Councillors John Evans (deputy chairman of the committee). W Roberts, Hulse,Davies,John Roberts,Emethurst,James Evans, Rogers, Tinnion,Brough, Malkin, Duncan, Bennett, Sampson, A Jolley, Ellison, and Baird. Several of the officials of the Corporation also attended. The party left Bank Howe in brakes at 2 o'clock and proceeded to the new works at High Warren, Appleton, where they inspected the new high level circular service reservoir now in course of construction which will have a Capacity of 1± million gallons. The object of this reservoir is to improve the supply to Stockton Heath and the surrounding district,where it has been found impossible hitherto to get sufficient pressure. Afterwards a visit was paid to the old works at Appleton where further extensions are shortly to be pro- ceeded with. These consist of the provision of two new filters and a service reservoir and the recon- struction of the e,.isting filters. Workmen's cottages will also be built here. Re-entering the conveyances the party pro- ceeded across the town to the new wells and pump- ing station in Delph-lane, Middleton. These exten- sions are being constructed from the designs and under the superintendence of Mr Dess at an estir mated cost of £247,000. The service reservoi accommodation is al&o being incieased by a new reservoir whose size is 215 feet by 190 feet and depth 20 feet. Its capacity is I} million gallons. The old and new works at Winwick were inspected with evident interes% The extension schemes were fully explainel by Councillor Evans and Mr Dees. the water engineer, and the visitors seemed satisfied that the future requirements of the borough will be adequately fulfilled, and that am- ple provision is being made to cope with the rapid growth of the town.

LATEST NEWS. The America Cup Race. Yesterday's Race Abandoned. The first day's race for the America Cup yesterday was abandoned owing to the yachts having exceeded the time limit.
